Recipe Index
“A great introduction to cultures is their cuisine. It not only reflects
their evolution, but also their beliefs and traditions.”
~ Vikas Khanna
Drinks
Lakshmi’s Special Cardamom Tea
Ingredients
4 Green whole cardamom pods
1 Cinnamon stick
1 TBPS Darjeeling or other black loose-leaf tea
2 Black whole peppercorns
1 TBPS Freshly grated ginger
4 Cloves
3 cups water
½ cup Milk (optional)
Sugar or honey to taste (optional)
Method
Grind spices in a mortar and pestle or electric spice grinder.
Combine all the ingredients (except milk and sweetener) in a pot and put on heat for a half hour.
Add milk if desired and boil another few minutes.
Strain and serve in your favorite tea cup, adding sugar and honey to your desired taste.
Madras Coffee
Ingredients
South Indian coffee filter
½ Cup boiling water
2 TBSP ground coffee
Hot milk to taste
Sugar to taste
Method
Add the ground coffee to the upper compartment of the coffee filter, spreading it with a spoon and then pressing down gently with the plunger.
Add the boiling water, cover with the lid and let the coffee percolate into the lower vessel for about 15 to 20 minutes.
For each serving, add about 2 to 3 TBPS (depending on how strong you prefer your coffee) of the decoction, sugar to taste, and hot milk.
The traditional serving vessel is known as the davara, consisting of a tumbler and saucer. The contents are poured back and forth between the tumbler and saucer to create a frothy coffee texture and release the aroma.
Main Dishes & Light Bites
Aval Upma
Ingredients
Aval
2 Cups of aval (flattened rice)
1 Medium onion, chopped
1 TSP of Ginger, grated
2 Green chilies (optional)
Salt to taste
Lemon juice (fresh, optional)
Temper
1 to 2 TBSP of Ghee (or oil)
1 to 2 Sprig(s) of curry leaves
½ TSP of Mustard seeds
3 TBSP of Roasted peanuts (preferable, otherwise other nuts can be used)
1 TSP Chana dal
¼ TSP of Turmeric powder
Method
Thoroughly rinse aval, drain out the water and set aside.
Heat the ghee and fry the peanuts until golden in hue. Remove for garnish later.
In the same pan, add in the chana dal and mustard seeds, frying until the dal is golden in color.
Sauté the onions, green chilies, curry leaves and ginger until the onions are translucent.
Add in the turmeric powder and salt. If the contents are dry, add a splash of water.
Add in the aval and still well. Then cover and cook on low heat until steamed.
To serve, add to a serving bowl and top with the roasted peanuts.
Aloo Gobi
Ingredients
2 or 3 TBSP of Ghee or oil
2 Medium potatoes (aloo), peeled, and cubed
2 Cups of cauliflower (gobi) florets, chopped
1 Medium onion, finely chopped
½ Inch of ginger, peeled and grated
3 Garlic cloves, peeled and minced
1 Green chili (optional)
Salt to taste
½ TSP Cumin seeds
¼ TSP of Turmeric powder
1 TSP of Coriander powder
½ TSP of Dried mango powder (amchur, optional)
Method
In a pan, heat ghee and add in cumin seeds.
Once the seeds sizzle, add in ginger, garlic and sauté for up to a minute.
Add in onions until translucent and not browned.
Then add in potatoes and green chili. Stir for a few minutes, then cover and continue to cook on low to medium heat until about half done. You can add in a bit of water to help the potatoes cook faster.
Add in the cauliflower and cook for a few minutes.
Add in the turmeric and coriander powder. Mix well, then add in 2 TBPS of water.
Cover and cook over low to medium heat until both the potatoes and cauliflower are tender.
Add in salt and cook a few more minutes on a lower flame.
At the end, sprinkle the dried mango powder and serve along rice or roti.
Punjabi Dal (Lentils) Tadka
Ingredients
1 Cup Toor dal or moong dal
3 Cups water
¼ TSP Turmeric powder
Salt to taste
Tempering (Tadka)
1 Medium onion, finely chopped
2 to 3 Green chilies, slit
1 TBSP Freshly grated ginger
4 to 5 Garlic cloves, minced
1 TSP Cumin seeds
2 Medium tomatoes, finely chopped
2 TBSP of Ghee or oil
Garnish (optional)
2 TBSP Fresh coriander leaves, chopped
Method
Wash dal thoroughly and soak for 30 minutes.
Boil the dal with water, turmeric, and a pinch of salt until soft and cooked through (about 20 to 25 minutes).
In a small pan, heat ghee or oil. Add cumin seeds and let them splutter.
Add chopped onions, ginger, and garlic. Sauté until onions turn golden brown.
Add chopped tomatoes. Cook until tomatoes soften and oil separates.
Pour this tempering (or tadka) over the cooked dal and mix well. Simmer for 5 minutes to let flavors combine.
Garnish with fresh coriander leaves and serve hot with rice or roti.
Chicken Biryani
Ingredients
1 Kilogram of chicken pieces
3 ½ Cups basmati rice
½ Kilogram of potatoes (boiled and cut in half)
For the chicken curry:
3 Large onions, sliced
2 ½ Cups of vegetable oil
Paste – blend following:
12 Green chillis
80 Grams of garlic
80 Grams of ginger
Garam masala
1 TSP of Black peppercorns
3 Large sticks of cinnamon
1 TSP of Cloves
4 to 6 Black cardamoms
3 Bay leaves
1 Cumin seeds
Additional ingredients
1 TBSP of Coriander powder
3 Large tomatoes, sliced
250 Grams of fresh yoghurt
½ TSP Nutmeg powder
½ TSP Mace
2 TBSP of Kasuri methi
For the rice
3 TBSP of Salt
3 TSP of Caraway seeds
2 Bay leaves
1 Large stock of cinnamon
6 Cloves
¼ TSP of Black peppercorns
2 Black cardamoms
5 Liters of water
1 TSP of Saffron soaked in ¼ cup of hot water
Couple of drops of kewra (optional)
Method
Chicken curry:
Brown the onions till golden brown in the oil.
Take out the onions out of the oil and add the chicken pieces to the same oil.
Then add the paste, coriander powder and the garam masala ingredients.
Stir fry for about 3 to 5 minutes, then cover and let cook in its own water on low heat.
When the chicken is half cooked, add in the tomatoes and cover. Let it cook for another five minutes.
When the tomatoes are cooked, then add in the fried onions, crushing the onions with your hands as you add them..
Add in the yoghurt, nutmeg, javitri and cook for another 2 minutes.
The curry is ready when the consistency still looks a bit runny.
Rice
Boil water with the rice and add the ingredients, except for the saffron and the kewra.
Assembly of biryani
In a large vessel, cover the bottom with 1 ½ cup of the boiled rice.
Then layer with the boiled potato halves.
Pour the chicken curry on the potatoes.
Sprinkle the kasuri methi over the curry.
Add the remaining rice on top.
Then pour the saffron water and kewra over the rice.
Cover tightly and cook till the chicken is done.
Dish up and serve.
Chicken Tikka Masala
Ingredients
Marinade
2 LBS of Bones chicken thighs, cubed
½ Cup of Greek yoghurt
½ TSP of Kashmiri red chili powder
¼ TSP of Ground black pepper
½ TSP of Cumin powder
1 TSP Coriander powder
¼ TSP of Green cardamom powder
¼ TSP of Cloves powder
¼ TSP of Cinnamon powder
1 TBSP of Freshly squeezed lemon juice
Salt to taste
2 to 3 Garlic cloves, grated
1 TBSP of Grated ginger
1 TBSP Oil
Tikka Masala
2 to 3 TBSP of Ghee or oil
2 Medium onions, chopped
1 Green chili, sliced long and deseeded (optional)
2 to 3 Garlic cloves, grated
1 TBSP of Grated ginger
½ TSP of Kashmiri red chili powder
1 TBSP of Coriander powder
1 TSP of Cumin powder
14 oz Can, no salt added peeled tomatoes
1 Cup of Hot water
½ Cup of Heavy cream, plus 2 TBSP for garnish
Method
Marinate Chicken
In a large bowl, put in cubed chicken after washing and drying off excess moisture with a paper towel. Gently prick chicken with a fork in one or two areas.
Combine all remaining marinade ingredients into a bowl and mix with chicken cubes thoroughly.
Cover the bowl and place in the fridge for a least 3 hours, and best if overnight.
Tikka Masala
Heat 1 TBSP of ghee in a pan over medium heat and add in onions when ghee is hot, sprinkling 1 TSP of salt. Sauté until the onions turn a deep golden, almost brown.
Add in garlic, ginger and green chili, continue to sauté for about a minute.
Reduce the heat to low and add in the dry spices and stir in.
Then add the tomatoes and cook on medium heat until the sauce thickens.
Pour in the cup of hot water, mix and bring to a rolling boil. Then cover and allow to thicken for 10 to 12 minutes.
Chicken Tikka Masala
In a separate pan, heat up 1 TBSP of ghee on medium heat. Add in the marinated chicken and sauté for a few minutes on each side and until almost browning / charred texture. (This can separately be
prepared on a grill for a more smokey flavor).
For the tikka masala, add in the cream and stir well, then add in the grilled chicken and cook up to 5 minutes, ensuring the chicken is soft and tender and the gravy is a thick consistency.
Taste test for salt, then transfer to a serving bowl and add the cream garnish before serving with rice, or naan.
Puri
Ingredients
1 Cup of atta (wheat flour)
1 TSP of Ghee or oil
½ TSP of Carom seed (ajwain, optional)
¼ Cup of water
Pinch of salt
Oil (specific to deep frying)
Method
Mix atta, ajwain, and salt in a large mixing bowl.
Slowly add in water and knead to create a stiff dough.
Add in the 1 TSP of oil and form a smooth ball. Keep dough covered and to the side. The dough should not be allowed to rest for too long, otherwise the puris will not come out puffy.
Divide into even portions (about eight small balls).
Heat up oil, dropping in a small test dough to check that it is hot enough.
Roll the dough balls into moderately thick circles.
Slide in the puris. Let the puris rise slightly, then using a perforated ladle, gently press the puri down to help it puff up.
Flip so that the other side also has a golden, crisp texture.
Remove and place on a few layered paper towels to absorb the excess oil. Do not stack.
Serve with chole, aloo gobi, or other side dish.
Coconut Chutney
Ingredients
1 Cup of grated coconut
2 TBSP of Roasted split chana dal
1 or 2 Cloves of garlic
½ TSP of Cumin powder
1/3 Cup of water
1 Green chili (optional)
Salt to taste
For tempering
1 TSP Ghee or oil
½ TSP Mustard seeds
1 Dried red chili (crushed gently, optional)
1 Sprig of curry leaves
Method
Grind the coconut, garlic, cumin seeds, green chili, salt, roasted chana and water in a blender into a coarse blend. Set aside.
In a pan, heat up the ghee on medium heat and add mustard seeds and dried red chili until the mustard seeds start to pop.
Add in the curry leaves and gently sauté for a minute. Then add to the blended mixture slowly.
Can be served as an accompaniment to meals like dosa and idli.
Chole
Ingredients
1 Cup Chickpeas (Chole), soaked overnight with a TSP of baking soda
3 Cups water
1 Medium onion, finely chopped
2 Medium tomatoes, finely chopped
2 to 3 Green chilies, slit
1 TBSP Freshly grated ginger
4 to 5 Garlic cloves, minced
1 TSP Cumin seeds
1 TSP Coriander powder
½ TSP Turmeric powder
2 TBSP Ghee or oil
1 Black tea bag (for color)
Salt to taste
Fresh coriander leaves, chopped for garnish (optional)
Method
Drain soaked chickpeas and boil with 3 cups water (about 30–40 minutes). Add the tea bag at the end to add color, then remove after 3 to 4 minutes.
In a small pan, heat ghee or oil. Add cumin seeds and let them splutter.
Add chopped onions, green chilies, ginger, and garlic. Sauté until onions turn golden brown.
Add chopped tomatoes, turmeric, and coriander powder. Cook until tomatoes soften.
Add this masala to the boiled chickpeas, mix well, and simmer 5–10 minutes. Adjust salt.
Garnish with fresh coriander leaves and serve hot with puri.
Dosa
Ingredients
½ Cup of Urad dal
1 to 2 Cups of cold water
1 ½ Cup of basmati rice
2 TBSP of Chana dal
½ TSP of Fenugreek seeds
2 TBPS of Flattened rice
Salt to taste
Ghee or oil, as needed
Method
Rinse the urad dal, chana dal, and fenugreek seeds in enough water at least 4 hours.
Separately, do the same with the rice.
Rinse and soak flattened rise in water for at least 30 minutes.
Drain the above ingredients.
Grind the above ingredients (except rice) until smooth and bubbly, then transfer to a large pot or container.
Separately, blend the rice until smooth and add to the batter.
Mix well and then cover to allow fermenting. Place in a warm place for up to 18 hours until light and bubbly.
To make dosas, portion out the amount of dosa batter you’d like to use and season with salt. Store the remainder in the refrigerator.
Heat a tawa on medium heat. Season with a bit of oil.
When hot, sprinkle a bit of water on the pan and ladle in about ¼ cup of batter. With the bottom of the ladle, quickly spread the batter evenly out, working from the center.
When you see the dosa browning, it is ready to flip. You can add ghee or oil as the dosa turns golden brown in color.
Repeat with remaining batter.
Serve dosas with coconut chutney, sambar and side dishes.
Idli
Ingredients
1 Cup of Urad daal
4 Cups Idli rice
2 TBSP of Fenugreek seeds
2 TBSP of Salt
Method
Thoroughly wash each ingredient, then soak for 3 to 5 hours in separate bowls. Make sure to wash the rice a few times to get the starch out and also for a softer and whiter idli color.
In a wet grinder, grind the soaked fenugreek seeds in water for a few minutes until a fine texture.
Drain the urad dal and grind in same wet grinder, slowly adding the ½ cup of water. Grind for around a half hour, then transfer the mixture into a bowl and set aside.
Drain the rice and the grind for a half hour until smooth, using water as needed to get a smooth texture.
Combine the rice batter with the dal mixture.
Add the salt and allow to ferment for at least 8 hours, if not overnight.
After the fermenting process and when ready to make the idlis, mix the batter again.
Add water in an idli vessel and place on medium heat.
Grease the idli plate and fill with batter, then gently placing the idli plate into the idli vessel.
Steam the idlis for about 6 to 8 minutes, keeping an eye on them to ensure they do not harden.
Remove the plate, leaving to cool for about 5 minutes, before taking out the individual idlis.
Serve the idlis hot, on their own or with sambar.
Jackfruit Curry
Ingredients
1 TBSP of Ghee or olive oil
2 TBSP of Black peppercorns
1 TBSP of Cumin seeds
1 TBSP of Coriander seeds
2 Bay leaves
2 Cloves of garlic, minced
1 TBSP Grated, fresh ginger
1 Diced medium onion
2 Diced medium tomatoes
1 Can of jackfruit (14 oz)
1 Can of coconut milk
1 ½ Cups of stock
1 TSP of Turmeric powder
Salt to taste
Method
In a spice grinder or with a mortar and pestle, gently grind the spices.
In a skillet, heat the ghee over medium heat and then add the ground spices and bay leaves.
After a couple of minutes, add onion and sauté until translucent. After about 5 to 7 minutes, add in the garlic and ginger and cook further for a couple of minutes. Then add in the tomatoes and cook down.
Drain the jackfruit and pull apart with your hands or a fork.
Add into the pan, along with turmeric and salt.
Cook for another 8 to 10 minutes and then add, coconut milk and stock.
After the sauce is thick and creamy (another 10 to 15 minutes), the dish is ready to serve with basmati rice.
Kerala Fish Fry
Ingredients
1 Large Pomfret
½ TBSP Red Chili Powder
1 TBSP Pepper Powder
¼ TSP Turmeric Powder
Salt to taste
2 TBSP Fresh Lemon Juice
1 Sprig of Curry Leaves
1 to 2 TBSP Coconut Oil
2 Fresh Lemon Slices to flavor at end
Onion Slices for garnish
Method
Clean pomfret and make slits on both sides.
Mix the red chili powder, pepper powder, turmeric powder, salt, and lemon juice
together, and then evenly coat the fish with it on both sides. Marinate for 30 minutes.
Heat oil in frying pan, and add curry leaves.
Place the fish carefully and shallow fry (use less oil) over medium heat until charred, then flip and do the same on the flip side.
Once cooked, place on a paper towel to absorb excess oil.
Then squeeze a lemon on top of the fish.
Serve with onion slices and rice.
Mushroom Fry
Ingredients
2 TBSP of Ghee or oil
2 Cups of White mushrooms, sliced
¼ Cup of Raw cashews
1 Small onion, finely chopped
¼ Cup of Unsalted crushed tomatoes or a small tomato, finely chopped or pureed
1 TSP of Garlic paste
1 TSP of Ginger paste
1 Green chili, sliced (optional)
5-7 Curry leaves
½ TSP Cumin seeds
¼ TSP of Red chili powder
½ TSP of Turmeric powder
1 TSP of Garam masala (optional)
Salt to taste
1 TSP of Lemon juice
Method
Soak cashews for 20 minutes, drain and then grind.
In a pan, heat up oil and then add in cumin seeds.
Once the seeds splutter, add in garlic and ginger paste and sauté until aromatic.
Add in onions and chili, frying till onions are brown.
Add in the tomatoes and cook down on medium heat.
Add in the spices (red chili powder, turmeric powder, garam masala), curry leaves and continue cooking for another 5 to 7 minutes.
Add in the mushrooms and ground cashew paste and cook for another 5 minutes.
Before serving, add lemon juice.
Enjoy with rice or roti.
Mutton or Lamb Curry
Ingredients
1 ½ Kilo of Mutton cubes (or leg of lamb, cut into large cubes)
1 Kilo of Onions, sliced
1 TSP of Whole black peppercorns
1 ½ TSP of Coriander seeds
15 Whole red chillis (optional)
1 Garlic pod, with cloves peeled and chopped
3” Piece of ginger, skin peeled
4 Black cardamoms
6 Green cardamoms
4-5 Pieces of cinnamon sticks
Salt to taste
1 Cup of oil
Method
Heat the oil in a large, heavy pan.
Fry the onions until transparent in color.
Make the garlic and ginger into a paste and add after the onions have been fried. Cook until the raw smell disappears.
Add in the meat and fry for about ten minutes.
Add rest of the spices and cook over a slow flame (adding in about ½ cup to 1 cup of water), stirring frequently till the meat is cooked and the gravy has reduced.
Add in the red chilies at the end so that they retain their color.
Serve hot with naan or roti.
Paneer parathas
Ingredients
Dough
2 Cups of Whole wheat flour
Pinch of Salt
1 TBSP Melted ghee
½ Cup Water (more as required)
Stuffing
1 Cup of Grated paneer
2 TBSP of Finely chopped coriander leaves
1 or 2 Cloves, minced (optional)
½ TSP of Roasted cumin powder
½ TSP of Dry ginger powder
Salt to taste
Frying
2 to 3 TBSP of ghee
Method
Dough
Combine flour, salt, ghee in a large bowl, then slowly add water and mix until soft but not sticky.
Let the dough rest for 30 minutes.
Then, knead for another 5 minutes.
Divide the dough into 8 portions.
Stuffing
In another bowl, combine the ingredients for the stuffing.
Divide into 8 portions.
Paratha
Roll dough with rolling pin until a small disc and place in palm. Feel free to use dry flour when rolling out, dusting off any excess, to prevent stickiness.
Place one of the stuffing portions in the center of the disc and pinch the dough closed around the stuffing, sealing the stuffing inside.
Flatten the dough ball, roll gently in dry flour, and create an 8-inch paneer paratha disc. You can dust the rolling pin to assist with rolling and to prevent the dough from sticking.
Place the paratha on a heated tawa or a frying pan and cook one side on medium
heat for a few minutes, then flip and repeat.
Add ghee to the top layer of the paratha and flip to cook that layer.
Do the same to the other layer of the paratha.
After a few minutes, when the paratha starts to turn a golden hue with some light brown spots on each side, remove and place on a plate.
Repeat steps to make up to 8 parathas.
Serve with butter, yoghurt, and pickle.
Papri Chaat (Easy Recipe)
Ingredients
Green chutney
2 Cups of Coriander leaves
1 Raw green mango, pip removed, cut and peeled
1 Cup of mint leaves
2 to 3 Green chilies
¼ Inch of ginger, peeled
1 Lemon, juiced
Salt to taste
Water, as needed
Tamarind chutney
½ Cup of Tamarind paste
½ Cup of Sugar (or jaggery)
½ TSP of Ginger powder
½ TSP of Roasted cumin powder
¼ TSP of Red chili powder
½ TSP of Coriander powder
Salt to taste
Water, as needed
Store-bought crisp papdi
1 Medium Potato, boiled and then chopped into small cubes
1 Can of Chickpeas, washed and drained
½ cup of Greek yoghurt or dahi
1 TBSP of Sugar
½ Cup of Aloo bhujia (e.g., Haldiram’s brand)
½ TSP of Roasted cumin powder
1 or 2 Pinches of red chili powder for decoration
Salt to taste
Method
To make green chutney, add all ingredients into a blender. Blend into a fine thick chutney, adding water as needed.
To make the tamarind chutney, add all ingredients into a blender. Blend into a fine thick chutney, adding water if needed.
Papdi Chaat Assembly – when ready to serve guests to avoid papdi from becoming soggy
In a bowl, beat together the yoghurt and sugar. Add green chutney and tamarind chutney to desired taste.
In a serving dish, place papdi. Top with the potatoes and chickpeas.
Then pour desired amount of the yoghurt blend on top on the papdi, potatoes and chickpeas.
Sprinkle some cumin powder and red chili powder.
Sprinkle a generous amount of aloo bujia
Ready to serve.
Rajma
Ingredients
1 Cup of Dried red kidney beans (or use canned 15-oz cans of red kidney beans)
2 TBSP of Oil (or ghee)
½ TSP of Cumin seeds
½ TSP of Turmeric powder
½ TSP of Coriander powder
1 Finely chopped onion
1 TSP Grated garlic cloves
1 TSP Grated ginger clove
1 or 2 Green chillis, deseeded and finely chopped (optional)
2 or 3 Chopped tomatoes (or 14-oz can of tomatoes, no salt added)
Salt to taste
Chopped coriander leaves and julienned ginger (optional garnish)
Method
Overnight, soak the dried red kidney beans. (Skip if using canned beans)
With 1 ½ cups of water, put in a pressure cooker and heat on medium heat until the pressure cooker whistles 3 times and the beans have softened but holding its shape.
(Please be mindful of pressure cooker safety and follow instructions of use).
In a pot, heat oil or ghee and add in the cumin seeds, turmeric powder, and coriander powder. Cook for a few minutes on medium heat until the aromatics are released.
Add in the onions and green chillis (if desired), and sauté until golden brown.
Add in the grated ginger and garlic and sauté for a couple of minutes.
Add in the tomatos and cook until this thickens.
Add in the red kidney beans with the water, mix, and continue cooking for another 15 minutes.
To thicken the gravy, mash some of the red kidney beans with the ladle against the pot during the cooking process using the side of the pot.
Add in salt to taste.
Garnish with chopped coriander leaves and julienned ginger (if desired) and serve hot with basmati rice.
Sambar
Ingredients
Dal
1 Cup of Toor dal, washed and rinsed
2 Cups of Water
1 TBSP of Tamarind paste
½ TSP of Turmeric powder
Salt to taste
1 Medium onion, diced
1 Large tomato, diced
1 Medium carrot, diced
2 to 3 Vegetable drumsticks, cut into 8 to 10 pieces
1 Green chili (optional)
Sambar and Sambar Powder
1 TBSP of Ghee or oil
1 TBSP of Coriander seeds
1 TBSP of Chana dal
1 TSP of Urad dal
½ TSP of Methi seeds
½ TSP of Cumin seeds
3 to 4 Dried red chilies
Temper
1 TBSP of Ghee or oil
1 Sprig of curry leaves
½ TSP of Mustard seeds
Method
Sambar Powder (alternatively, you can skip this step and use 2 TBSP of readymade powder bought from the store)
Dry roast the red chilies and the dals on low to medium heat until golden.
Add in the coriander seeds and fry for a few minutes. Then remove into a bowl to cool.
Add in methi seeds and coriander seeds and once aromatic, remove and add to same bowl.
Once cool, grind the dry roasted ingredients into a fine powder.
Dal
In a pressure cooker (use with caution and an understanding of how to use the cooker), add the drained toor dal and 2 cups of water and allow to cook on medium heat for 2 to 4 whistles.
Set aside, and once cooled, mash the daal until it has a smooth consistency.
Sambar
In a pot, add 1 TBSP of ghee and cook on medium heat. After it heats up, add in the onion and saute for another minute.
Add in the remaining vegetables and cook for another few minutes.
Add 4 cups of water and once this boils, take ¼ cup of the hot water into a separate bowl. In that bowl, add tamarind paste, mix and set aside.
When the vegetables are al dente, add in the turmeric, sambar powder and salt.
Next, add in the tamarind water.
Continue cooking for a few more minutes, then add the dal and stir into the sambar mixture.
Bring to a boil.
Temper
In a small pan, heat up 1 TBSP of ghee and add in all the temper ingredients except the curry leaves.
Add the curry leaves once the spices pop and cook till the leaves crispen.
Then take the temper and pour it while hot to the sambar.
Serve with rice, idli or dosa.
Vegetable Stew
Ingredients
1 TBSP Ghee or oil
1 Cinnamon stick
2-3 Green cardamon pods
2-3 Cloves
1 Small onion, thinly sliced
1 Small bunch of green beans, chopped
1 Small potato, chopped into small cubes
¼ Cup of Green peas (can be frozen)
1 Small carrot, chopped
3-5 Julienned ginger strips
8-10 Cauliflower florets
3-5 Curry leaves
1 Cup of Water
2 Cups of Thinned coconut milk
¼ Cup of Thick coconut milk
1 Green chili, thinly chopped (optional)
Salt to taste
Method
In a large pot, heat up the ghee and sauté the spices (i.e., cinnamon, cardamom, cloves) until it releases an aroma.
Then, add in the onion and cook till translucent but not brown.
Add in the vegetables (beans, peas, carrot, ginger, cauliflower florets,) and sauté for a few minutes.
Add in the water, salt, chili, and curry leaves and combine.
Cover and boil for 5 minutes, then add the thin coconut milk.
Cook for another 5 to 7 minutes, until the vegetables are cooked.
Remove from heat, add in the thick coconut milk and serve with a bowl of rice.
Desserts
Coconut barfi
Ingredients
3 Cups of Coconut, freshly grated
2 Cups of Sugar
½ Cup of Milk
¼ TSP Ground green cardamom
Method
On a medium flame, add the grated coconut, sugar and milk in a large wok.
Mix and continue to stir as the sugar dissolves and the mixture begins to thicken (about 15 to 25 minutes).
The mixture will begin to separate from the wok, at which point, continue to mix so that the mixture starts to come together.
Add in the cardamom powder.
On a greased baking dish or tray or one line with parchment paper, evenly spread the
Allow this to cool and set, then cut into rectangular serving pieces
Serve and enjoy or keep in the fridge for later snacking.
Payasam
Ingredients
2 TBSP Ghee
¾ Cup of Vermicelli (unroasted)
½ Cup of Sugar
½ TSP of Ground whole green cardamom
Pinch of Saffron soaked in 1 TBSP of hot water
4 Cups of Milk
Roasted chopped cashews, as desired
Method
Add the ghee to a pan and heat over a medium flame.
Roast the vermicelli till a lightly golden hue on a low medium flame.
Add milk and boil on same level of heat, occasionally stirring to ensure the vermicelli doesn’t burn at the bottom of the pan.
Add sugar, ground cardamom, and infused saffron water.
Cook down until the payasam thickens, but can still be poured out.
As the payasam cools, it will thicken further. If this will be set aside, cover with
a plastic wrap that touches the top layer of the payasam, so that a thick layer doesn’t form on top.
Serve either as a hot or chilled dish and top with cashews (if desired).
Kulfi
Ingredients
6 to 8 Kulfi molds (alternatively, popsicle molds)
1 Cup of Ground roasted, unsalted nuts (e.g., almonds, pistachios)
1 1/2 Cups of Condensed milk
1 1/2 Cups of Evaporated cardamom milk
Alternatively, you can use plain evaporated milk and add in 3-5 ground green cardamom pods.
1 1/3 Cups of Thickened cream (alternatively, use heavy cream)
Method
Mix all the ingredients (except nuts) together, making sure the mixture is free of any lumps.
Add in nuts and stir. Then fill molds.
Place in freezer and enjoy once frozen.
Mysore Pak
Ingredients
1 Cup of Gram flour
1 ¼ Cup of Sugar
½ Cup of Water
1 Cup of Ghee
Method
Grease a small tray or dish with some ghee (around 1 TBSP).
Sift the gram flour into a large bowl, then repeat.
Divide the flour into 3 small bowls and set aside.
In a deep frying pan, heat ghee on medium heat.
In a separate pot, add water and then sugar heating in medium heat and stirring until viscous and stringy.
Once that consistency is reached and the ghee remains hot on the other burner, add one small bowl of gram flour into the sugar syrup and cook on medium. Stir the flour well.
After this, add in the next bowl and repeat the process and then the last bowl.
Next add in one ladle of hot ghee into the flour mixture. The ghee must sizzle so it is important to make sure the ghee is hot.
Repeat this process until all the ghee is stirred into the flour mixture and has been absorbed.
When the mysore pak mixture looks thick and bubbly, it is done and there is no need to add anymore ghee.
Next transfer the mixture into the greased dish and set aside to cook for 15 minutes.
Once cooled, cut into desired portion sizes.
Serve and store any leftovers in a air tight container at room temperature.
Punjabi Poora
Ingredients
1 Cup of Whole wheat flour
2 TSP of Roughly ground anise or fennel seeds
1/3 Cup of Sugar
¼ TSP of Baking soda
Water, as required
Method
Combine the dry ingredients and mix.
Then, add water as needed to create a thick batter consistency.
On a medium flame, heat a non-stick pan on a medium flame and spread ghee on it.
In the heated pan, pour a batch of the batter and spread it into a circular shape.
Once a golden-brown color from the caramelization of the sugar, flip to the other side adding a bit more ghee.
Once the second side is of a similar color, it is ready to serve.
Rasgulla
Ingredients
Chenna
4 Cups of Whole milk
2 or 3 TBSP of White vinegar or lemon juice
4 Cups of Ice cold water
Cheese cloth
Sugar syrup
4 Cups of Water
4 Green cardamom pods, lightly crushed
1 Cup of White sugar
Method
Chenna
Bring milk to a boil.
Add in vinegar or lemon juice and stir till the milk curdles, switching off the heat.
After a couple of minutes, add in cold water and then drain the curdled milk in a colander lined with a cheese cloth.
Rinse out the acidic flavor and then, tightly squeeze and hand for a few hours. The texture at the end should be crumbly.
Rasgulla balls
Knead the crumbly chenna into a workable dough and then, stop kneading.
Create small balls, about fifteen, and set aside.
Sugar syrup
Boil the sugar, lightly crushed cardamom pods and water in a large pot, until sugar has dissolved.
Once a rolling boil, remove the cardamom pods, add in the balls gently, cover the pot, and cook for about 10 minutes, checking in on them at the 5 minute mark to gently stir the syrup. Avoid disturbing the balls.
After the 10 minutes, remove from the head and keep covered for another 20 minutes.
Check in to see if they’ve doubled in size and then, allow to cool and then chill before serving.
Ras Malai
Ingredients
Chenna
5 Cups of Full fat milk
1 to 2 TBSP of Vinegar
Cheesecloth
Syrup
1 Cup of White sugar
3 Cups of Water
½ TSP of green cardamom powder
Rabri
3 Cups of Full fat milk
1/3 Cup of Sugar
Pinch of saffron
Method
Rabri
Bring milk to boil. Once boiling, add sugar and saffron.
Reduce heat to medium and continue cooking, stirring every couple of minutes.
Keep cooking until reduces to half its quantity and then set aside to cool.
Split into two portions, with one portion to cool at room temperature and the other in the fridge.
Chenna
Bring milk to boil and then switch off.
Add 1 TBSP of vinegar and stir, which should start the curdle process.
If curdling doesn’t happen, switch on stove and cook on low to medium heat until curdling starts.
Once this process begins, add into a cheese cloth over a colander.
Wrap the chena and wash in running water to remove the vinegar taste.
Squeeze out water and hang for 1 hour to drain excess whey.
After an hour, transfer the chenna to a surface and knead into a smooth ball. Then, split into 12 balls and flatten into discs.
Sugar syrup
Bring water to boil, then add in the sugar until it dissolves. Then add in the cardamom powder.
Add in the discs and cook for 10 minutes on a medium to high heat.
Turn the stove off and remove the pot from the heat, cover with a lid, and allow to soak in the syrup for 20 minutes.
Rasmalai
Remove the discs and gently squeeze out excess syrup.
Add this to the warm portion of the rabri. Allow to rest for 4 hours.
When read to serve, add in chilled rabri.
Garnish with chopped nuts (e.g., pistachios).
Rava Kesari Bhath
Ingredients
¼ Cup of Ghee
¼ Cup of Semolina
1 Cup of Water
¼ to ½ Cup of Sugar depending on desired sweetness
Pinch of Saffron soaked in 1 TBSP of boiling water
¼ TSP of Ground green cardamom powder
Roasted Cashews and raisins for garnish (optional)
Method
Heat ghee on medium flame in a saucepan and once heated, add in the semolina. Roast until a golden-brown in color.
Add in water to the roasted semolina and boil, then reduce heat to a low flame and continue to stir.
Add in sugar, saffron water, and ground green cardamom powder, continuing to stir until the mixture begins to separate from pan.
Serve hot or cold, garnishing with nuts and raisins or strands of saffron.
Shrikand
Ingredients
2 Cups of Greek yoghurt
Pinch of Saffron strands
3 Cardamom seeds, ground
¼ Cup of Powdered sugar
2 to 3 TBSP Chopped nuts, if desired (e.g., roasted almonds, pistachios, etc.)
Method
Strain yoghurt in a cheese cloth, squeezing out any whey, and then wrap the cloth and hang for ½ hour.
Place the hung yoghurt in cheese cloth in a strainer over a large bowl. Then place a heavy object to help drain away the rest of the liquid out into the bowl below, ensuring the yoghurt doesn’t contact the liquid in the bowl below.
Transfer the bowl into the fridge overnight.
Soak the saffron in 1 TBSP of hot water for a few minutes.
Add saffron, powdered sugar and ground cardamom to the yoghurt and whisk, until smooth.
Place in fridge to chill, then add chopped nuts before serving.
Unniyappam
Ingredients
1 Cup of Rice flour
1 Medium-sized banana, mashed
1 ½ TBSP of Semolina flour
¾ Cup of Jaggery
1/2 TSP Black sesame seeds
1 TBSP Ghee
2 TBSP Grated coconut
3 TSP Ground green cardamom powder
1/2 Cup of Water (or as needed) and 2 TBPS of water
¼ TSP of Baking soda
Pinch of salt
Oil or Ghee for frying
Method
Combine rice flour, banana, baking soda, salt, and ground green cardamom powder in a bowl. Then add in water to make a smooth batter.
In a saucepan, dissolve jaggery in 2 TBSP of water on medium heat until thick consistency.
Cool jaggery syrup and then add to batter made earlier, mixing until a thick batter consistency.
In a small saucepan, heat up 1 TBSP of ghee and roast the grated coconut until golden brown.
Add golden brown grated coconut into the batter along with the black sesame seeds.
In an appe pan, pour oil or ghee halfway into each mould in the appe pan.
When oil or ghee is hot, spoon in batter and cook on one side. Flip and brown the other side and then remove with a toothpick.
Place on paper towels. Serve hot or cold.

For my James,
who never stops believing in me and
inspiring me to be a better version of myself.

Chapter 1 – Pride or Mada
“Comparison is the thief of joy.”
~ President Theordore Roosevelt
Before the sun rises in a small village near Idukki, Lakshmi tends to the wood fire she’s started so she can make a large batch of rice for the day’s meal. The rice will feed her father, brother, cousin-brother, mother and grandmother, covering their two meals of the day: breakfast and dinner. Dinner is usually accompanied with lentils or a small vegetable dish. With her small hands, Lakshmi then sweeps the two-room house, before heading out to the village well to gather the day’s water ration.
On her way back, she would have just enough time for herself to run out into the field of flowers. She sits there for just fifteen or twenty minutes and pulls out a scrap of paper and a pencil from her worn crossbody satchel to sketch the flowers surrounding her. She loves to create pictures of her surroundings on whatever scrap paper she can find. The dew on the flowers glisten as they are met with the sun’s warm, morning rays. Sometimes, Lakshmi would dry the petals of the grand lilac Gangalee flowers, crush them to create pigmented water, and then with her tiny fingers paint the vibrant purple petals, bringing the blossoms alive in her drawings. The mighty Gangalee flower stretches to about the size of her small palm with large boat-shaped petals layered around in a saucer shape, centered by a bright yellow stamen.
If her parents hadn’t chosen Lakshmi as her name, she was assured that her mother would have called her Gangalee for she adored the flowers. In the springtime, the fields would be covered in these glorious blooms and bees would feast on its delicious pollen, creating an aromatic honey notable to the region. Despite its beauty and allure, the Gangalee seeds were
toxic to humans and animals. Gangalee was truly a unique godly creation adorning this small village on the outskirts of Idukki hills; so gentle in appearance, but potentially deadly to the unsuspecting.
Once home, she made sure that all her chores were done before her father, younger brother, and cousin-brother were up. Her father happened to be there that morning. Usually, he spent most of his time on the road transporting scrap metal wherever the job required him to go. Her brother and cousin-brother, both 12 years old at the time, would head out to school as they did every morning. Lakshmi’s parents could only afford to send two of the children to school. When her aunt and uncle died in a bus accident the year before, her parents made the decision to send the boys over her. It was an easy and logical decision for them. She was 14 years old at the time and would be of marriageable age in a year. She was slender, stunning, but most of all, had the desired fair complexion. When she was born, her parents named her Lakshmi, not only the revered Goddess of Prosperity, but because the Goddess herself was thought to have emerged out of a churning ocean of milk. Her name could not have been more fitting in their minds.
It made Lakshmi very sad when her parents told her she would no longer attend school. She was a bright student, always eagerly reading books, writing stories and illustrating them with her drawings. She loved learning about different subjects, with history being one of her favorite subjects. She could spend hours studying and reading if given the opportunity.
Her grandmother was the only one who ever seemed to notice her love for books and learning. She had tried hard to convince Lakshmi’s parents to sacrifice them spending money on her medication so that Lakshmi could finish at least 10
th
class but to no avail. Lakshmi’s parents saw no point in creating a learned
girl from a small, indistinct village, in the middle of Kerala. Besides, who would want to marry a girl more educated than the groom?
Soon after Lakshmi dropped out of school, her mother, Devi, started taking her to work with her on the cardamom estate perched on the hills of Idukki. Devi had worked there all her life. She joined her mother there when she was just 10 or 11 years old, never having had the opportunity to go to school herself either. She was now in her mid-30s and couldn’t read or write very well anymore. She could, however, expertly select which pods to pluck during harvesting and the estate owner highly regarded her talent and work ethic around this. Devi was proud of this talent and had risen in the ranks, where she would train other women on the Idukki Eco Farm estate to do the same. With her skills, the estate produced some of the most fragrant, citrusy pods that were highly sought after in the spice markets around the world.
Early, every morning, Lakshmi would sit in a dingy, open-air pickup that would come to collect her, her mother and a number of the village ladies. The driver would turn on the local Malayalam radio station blasting the latest movie songs as they traversed up the hills to the estate. The women would laugh and gossip as the truck would veer through the windy roads covered on either side with luscious palms and flora. What Lakshmi didn’t realize at the time was the nostalgia she would feel years later when she would reminiscence about these days. At a future time, she would look back fondly and remember the air and how sweet it would smell, but also the sticky feeling as her linen clothes clung to her body in the heat as they would ride to the plantation through the dense greenery.
Lakshmi missed going to school, but she also enjoyed the time she spent with the women at the back of the pick-up truck.
She especially loved the everyday gossip. The story of that one girl of the village with loose morals. Why Shanta couldn’t get pregnant. Whose mother-in-law won the
wickedest witch to put it politely
title. She also took pleasure from her time toiling away at the plantation alongside her mother. There were a few other younger girls worming there alongside their mothers as well. In fact, generations of women had been employed here, chattering away to pass time as they plucked and sorted the best cardamom pods. Nothing ever changed here – women married very young, giving birth as teens, and continuing on as day laborers at estates like these.
The Idukki Eco Farm, however, was the best employer in the region for this unskilled agricultural job. Every year the inspectors would visit the estate to ensure the practices adhered with international guidelines. Without fail, the inspectors always gave their seal of approval and the estate proudly stamped “Fair trade” on their product.
The estate owners truly deserved the rating and never needed to grease the palms of the inspectors as other employers were known to do. They had always been equitable and generous employers in the community. For generations now, they continued to pay fair wages and that way, they kept a loyal crop of workers which gave them a competitive advantage in the market. It was one of the best places to work in the area and Devi was very pleased to have secured a spot for her daughter. In Devi’s mind, it would make her more marketable to potential grooms when she became of marriageable age over the next year. Survival was core to the villagers and having job security at the Idukki Eco Farm was a valuable asset to have.
On the plantation, the owners had built a grand multigenerational chalet style home. Every Sunday afternoon, the young girl laborers were invited to join the youngest child of
the estate owners on the patio for tea parties. The heiress would dress up in elegant western dresses and sit tall in a wicker chair near the lily pond, shaded by the large awning of the chalet. Five or so of the village girls would sit on the ground in front of her, dressed in their navy salwar and a matching kameez embroidered with “Idukki Eco Farm” stitching in white on the front pocket. They marveled with wide eyes at Ms. Kanika’s fancy chiffon, taffeta or silk dresses that she would acquire on shopping trips to Kochi, Bombay or when in the Middle East.
One afternoon, Ms. Kanika was dressed in a rose pink chiffon frock. Her dark brown hair tied back in a ponytail held together with a matching ribbon. Her eyes were a beautiful dark green hazel, and her soft blush lips complemented her unblemished fair skin.
“Aya, aaaaya,” Ms. Kanika would call out to her maid. “Time for tea and shortbread biscuits. Aaaya, where are you? My friends and I are waiting.”
A few moments later, Mamta came out with a large tray of freshly brewed cardamon tea with a punch of the ground spice lingering in the air. These afternoon teas were always a treat that Lakshmi and the girls looked forward to, especially after spending the week harvesting crop in the fields. It wasn’t clear if the afternoon teas were held so that Ms. Kanika had company. She did seem lonely as the only daughter of the household.
Unlike her much older brothers, Hari and Rattan, she wasn’t allowed to play outside. Her mother was very strict around Kanika’s beauty regiment and an undesirable tan was out of the question. The village girls never really saw any of Ms. Kanika’s friends come over to the house, but they never questioned it either. They were just glad for this weekly luxury afforded to them as lowly village girls. At home, they would receive just food
scraps of what was left-over after their grandfathers, fathers, brothers, and other male relatives ate.
Mamta poured the freshly brewed tea out of the floral bone China tea pot imported from England into one elegant cup decorated with English roses for Ms. Kanika. She then poured the remaining tea into steel cups for the village girls whose calloused hands were not suited for the dainty tea ware. Each of the girls were allowed as many soft, buttery biscuits as they wanted until gone.
When the girls were a bit younger, they would gather around Ms. Kanika over tea, munching away on cookies while Ms. Kanika enlightened them about Barbie dolls and cartoons. But as the girls matured, the conversation matured with Ms. Kanika sharing the plot of young British teenage girls at Malory Towers or the latest gossip at Ms. Kanika’s private English curriculum school.
All sorts of taboo subjects would be discussed under the awning of Ms. Kanika’s family home. Did the girls know that they should be getting their periods already or soon? Ms. Kanika was expecting hers any day now. In fact, her own mother had told her that she had gotten it around her age and so Ms. Kanika should expect the same.  As Ms. Kanika clambered on, the girls didn’t even know what she spoke of as she described how their bodies were changing, why their chests would grow into two bulbous lumps that men would go dizzy over, and how the girls themselves would transform into young women just like their own mothers once did. Until the mostly one-sided conversations with Ms. Kanika, the girls had never quite realized they were slowly becoming young women. They never even talked about these things amongst themselves. But when could they? When rushing to get chores done at home? Or while on their fields when working side by side their mothers?
~
One day on the way to work, Lakshmi’s ears were burning with chatter directed at her. She was two weeks away from her fifteenth birthday and she had come into womanhood beautifully. Unlike late bloomers, she had been endowed with much more than had been expected and early on. She stood in sharp contrast to her own mother who stood a flat five foot tall and was thin and unshapely as a matchstick at thirty-five years young.
On the other hand, Lakshmi towered over her by at least four inches and had developed beautiful breasts and shapely hips. In her salwar kameez, the outline of her perfectly slender frame would tease the village men as she walked to and from the water well in the early mornings before her work day began at the Idukki Eco Farm. Their shameless eyes would dance all over her comely, youthful body and they would salivate over her gorgeously, thick locks tied in a perfect braid as it cascaded down her spine. As she walked to the well and back every morning, she could feel their lewd thoughts penetrate her with their dirty stares. These were men she had grown up in front. Men she called uncle or brother.
“You need to get her married soon, Devi,” the women chirped at Lakshmi’s mother.
“She cannot be a burden to your household any longer. And look how beautiful she is with her pure milk skin. Plump, delicious. Her fruit is ripe and it is a good time for her to be wed. Any longer and she’ll be a dried-up mango,” said another as she looked Lakshmi up and down.
“Don’t wait till she is too old like Aditya’s daughter,” the lady with the crow’s feet and tightly tied bun added.
“Hahn, Hahn. I remember. So conceited her mother was. Such a beautiful, fair and tall girl with thick black hair. Just beaming with big breasts, tight bum, just like Lakshmi,” a thinly lipped woman responded laser focused on Aditya’s physicality.
“Didn’t get married till twenty-five and to that pauper’s son, Anil. No one wanted her then – chi, chi, chi,” the lady with the crow’s feet and tightly tied bun continued on with a grimace on her face.
“Have you seen her husband? A miner and his face dark as the iron from the quarry. Have you seen their children – two dirty little rascal girls. She ruined their futures too,” the thinly lipped woman couldn’t stop herself.
“Milk and dirt. No wonder her babies came out looking like monkeys,” the first woman sneeringly added.
And as the conversation continued disparaging the failed women of the village, Lakshmi’s mind began to wander as she glanced into the lush, green hills blanketed in the morning mist. The air was so fresh and crisp that every time she breathed in, her whole body felt revitalized. And as the day progressed, the morning mist slowly and silently lifted, opening the way for the sky so the sun shone through brightly.
Lakshmi thought about her future as a fifteen-year-old woman. She contemplated what it meant to be of marriageable age. Was she ready for her own family? A family that she would walk to the well in the morning for to get the day’s water ration. To cook batches of rice for. To tidy the house for.
Even though she had done this routine all her life, it was still the only dream that Lakshmi knew she could have. It was the same aspiration of her mother, her mother’s mother, and her mother before then. Each woman was married off to young men from the village or surrounding areas. Like Lakshmi was presently,
these women had worked the cardamom fields from dawn to dusk, chattering away with other women and sharing similar stories. This was their dream and now it was Lakshmi’s.
She definitely didn’t want to end up like Aditya’s daughter. She didn’t want to bring shame to her very proud mother and family. Plus, she didn’t want to have dirty children that looked like monkeys. She asked Goddess Lakshmi to forgive her for saying such things, but to bless her so she would not have to suffer that fate.
Lakshmi also understood her responsibilities as her parents’ only daughter very well. She was grateful for having been born a golden fair hue so that her mother never had to hear the damnation of the villagers for birthing a dark child. They had been so cruel to Kali. “Her mother named her right, Kali,” they would say and the village children would tease the dark-skinned girl. Yet, Kali had the most captivating eyes with the thickest of eyelashes that were lost in the shadow of her skin tone. And not knowing anything else for herself, Lakshmi prayed fervently to the Goddess who she was named after, asking the deity to bless her with a husband within that year. A husband so fantastic that all the village women’s jaws would drop to the ground with jealousy. Instead of insults, her family would need to fend of constant praises disguising their envy.
~
Lakshmi’s dainty beauty was well-known across the village and as she blossomed into that young woman, she inadvertently became the center of every mother’s desire. A rare lotus flower that had bloomed in the lowest echelons of Kerala society.
“Goddess Lakshmi brought me herself to me when you were born,” Lakshmi’s mother would croon as she helped me braid
my hair. “Every child before you would die in my womb within the first 8 weeks. Then Her Mata Rani blessed me with you, my beautiful, golden child. Not long after, Her All Mighty showered us with Dhruv. You are destined for greatness, my diamond.” As she said this, she pressed her finger on her lower eyeline heavy in kohl and transported the black spot behind my left ear to ward off the evil eye.
Laksmi’s mother lived for society and its ideals. She felt redeemed as a village woman following the births of her two children. Until then, she was the subject of mockery and ensured the brunt of insults for having multiple miscarriages. She reached a desperate point in her life and sought out spiritual remedies to help her cope. She did pujas, fasts, drank potions prescribed by the village mystic, and even went so far as to drank a thimble of cow’s urine under a full moon for 3 months before Lakshmi was finally conceived under the mystic’s guidance. She was happy not to have to hear the cackle of judgement any longer.
“Why did Arvind marry such a flat-chested woman? No figure. No children,” a village woman would croak.
“Do you remember Meenakshi’s girl? What a beauty with those womanly curves. Arvind’s mother was such a fool to reject that proposal. Now Meenakshi has five strapping grandsons now. Not one daughter,” another chimed in.
After finally reaching motherhood, Lakshmi’s mother finally felt like a complete and worthy woman.
~
The village was small and located on the outskirts of Idukki’s green hills endowed with cardamom and tea estates. Most everyone in the village knew one another for generations now.
Not many had left this close-knit community, except those with the means to do so. When the more well-off left for a better life, they never returned to invest their fortune into the village’s growth.
At the lowest echelon of society, you had those responsible for collecting human excrement from each home in baskets to take far away. Generation after generation, these families served the village as the clandestine sewer system delaying the need for plumbing infrastructure. When passing a dalit, no one would acknowledge their existence. Their caste lived far away from the village to keep their filthy existence away from the civilized classes. The poor souls were stuck in their karmic catch-22. They were branded for life from the day they were born as untouchable. They crept in the shadows taking care of the excrement that couldn’t be differentiated no matter whether you hailed from the priest class or came from their lowly class. Yet, this division was acceptable and once a dalit, always a dalit.
Above the dalits, came the caste to which Lakshmi and her family belonged to. The laboring class. The had various occupations, but the majority of them worked in the nearby quarries, farms, or for the trucking businesses that transported scrap or other such cargo. Rarely did one climb the ladder into the next rung on society’s ladder, but this had been possible marriage or an entrepreneurial opportunity. Most were content with this fate. Unlike the untouchables, they were afforded at least some respect and pride in the village.
Next, came the tradesmen. They had specialized skills and bargained for the best prices for goods and services offered in the village market. Their opinion mattered considerably to the village council and that in itself, deserved a certain elevated level of respect. If you were born into this class, you were easily given access to luxury and deference. The tradesmen
had spacious homes and for the wealthier of the lot, this meant household help. With their wealth and prestige, came influence in all matters to be considered, whether with regard to morality, lawfulness, or equity. A quiet whisper and some grease from a tradesman could easily change the otherwise seemingly unwavering stance of the upper ranking members of the village council.
Finally, the top strata of the village society: the village council and their families. The village council was composed of the most distinguished and wealthiest members. These were individuals born into families with the closest celestial ties. The villagers would lower their heads in unchallenged respect when passing a member or relation of the village council. The were the giants of society and automatically appeared better-looking, better-dressed, better fed and better bred in every way. Within the village council itself, there were layers of influence. Those with priest status and who sat nearest in line to the heavenly gods themselves, were at the very top. Through birth, they had the highest perch in the village and looked down on every other villager. At present, Ramdev’s father sat at the very top of the village council in this little village, nestled outside the Idukki hills.
~
Ramdev, the oldest son of the village elder, was in his early fifties and always dressed in the finest linens and custom-made sandals. His ancestral home was the largest and most luxurious of them all. It sat at a distance from every other family’s home, adding to its prestige. They had their own well to ensure their water remained pure. The house also had a lavish courtyard with a Tulsi plant blessing the center of the home. As with many of the village families, the house had entertained multi-generations
under the one roof. Unlike most village homes, however, they had a separate servants’ quarters for the live-in cook and driver.
The family also had two cows, Dumki and her baby, Chumki. Ramdev always started his day with a glass of fresh milk from the sacred animal and prepared by his wife, Gayatri. The milk was gently heated with a pinch of fragrant saffron, the world’s most expensive spice and jaggery. Many years ago, a tradesman traveling through their village had offered up the spice in return for a favor from Ramdev and after tasting the subtle floral notes, Ramdev made saffron a staple in their home. If Ramdev’s day did not start with a perfectly prepared glass of saffron-infused milk, he would have a terrible toddler-like tantrum. He would then complain that the rest of the day would now be unlucky. If things did go awry that day, Gayatri and the household would have to hear his griping till late into the evening.
Ramdev was born into the finest lineage of the priest class and having been so, acted without humility. If Ramdev wanted something, he only had to point and take. It was his celestial right. Gayatri, his wife, was a stunning young woman in her day and had been sixteen when she was betrothed to Ramdev, who had seen her in the village and demanded his mother he wanted to take her as his wife. Ramdev was enamored by Gayatri’s beauty. Her skin was a light golden hue and her eyes were the color of amber. Like Lakshmi, Gayatri had once held the spot of the most sought-after maiden in the town. Those captivating eyes intrigued the villagers and her peers envied her for it. But today, that fire that once lit up her face and fascinated the villagers, had slowly dulled with time.
Gayatri had been a dutiful wife per society’s standards. Eventually. For a long stretch and tumultuous time, she had trouble conceiving, much like Lakshmi’s mother. She had to bear the constant abuse and taunts from her in-laws and the
villagers. Worst of all, she endured the near nightly beatings of a drunk Ramdev that happened behind closed doors. Everyone in the household knew what was happening, but no one spoke of it. It was acceptable, especially since Gaytri had driven Ramdev into this desperation.
For now, Ramdev’s father had grasp of the village council. Ramdev had feared that he would lose the title and inheritance if Gayatri didn’t bear a son soon. Ramdev would lose everything to his conniving younger brother, Ravi, whose wife had given them not one, but three male heirs. The eldest was Shiva and then two younger twins, Ram and Lakshman all within the first three years of their marriage. Gayatri’s infertility frustrated Ramdev, causing his drunkenness and violence towards Gayatri.
At twenty, Gayatri finally gave birth to their first and only child. And fortunately, it was a son. They named their son after the God of Preservation and the Protector of the Universe, Vishnu. Upon his birth, the family held a lavish celebration in the village and Ramdev blessed every home with sweet shrikand, delicately flavored with the opulent saffron.
Once Vishnu was born, the tone of Ramdev, the household and the village changed. Everyone assumed now with the arrival of the heir, all the beatings would stop. Even Gayatri believed this would be the case and this was true up until Ramdev’s realization that Gayatri couldn’t get pregnant again. Even having secured an heir, Ramdev remained jealous of his brother who had been blessed with multiple sons. Even though everyone had moved on from gossiping about him and Gayatri’s relationship, Ramdev was still stuck in his envious ruminations. The frequency of the beatings diminished, but the intensity was unpredictable. Gayatri, like many of the village women, accepted this behavior from their husbands. Gayatri was just pleased that
she had fulfilled her duty as a wife and woman and the village chatter had shifted away from her now.
~
Being that Vishnu was their only child and the child of the wealthiest family in the village, he was thoroughly spoiled and was never told, “No”. Having been blessed with a son, Ramdev and Gayatri saw no reason not to give him that extra special license to do whatever he wanted. So, as Vishnu grew older, he became more and more entitled and disrespectful. He was nothing like his namesake in form or in action. Nevertheless, the villagers turned a blind eye, which only exacerbated his behavior as he got older.
“Boys will be boys,” the village women would justify when he was younger.
“He’s his mother’s only child. What else would one expect? He’ll grow out of these boyish ways,” another excused.
But as Vishnu became a teenager, he did not grow out of his mean-spirited habits. He only worsened in character. He grew more heartless and colder with age. He formed a gang of bullies that would harass the younger boys. He would leer at the young girls and make unsavory remarks about their bodies. He would even catch small birds, pin them to the tree, and slowly watch them die. He was never reprimanded for any of this behavior, which only further enabled him.
“When he’s married in a few years, you just see, his wife will set him straight,” the village women reasoned.
But his cruelty and unkindness did not sit well with his grandfather as Vishnu grew into a young man. To him, he was Ravana, the devil, in Lord Vishnu’s disguise. He stood in stark contrast to his eldest cousin-bother, Shiva, and this difference
did not go unnoticed. Their grandfather had observed both boys as they grew up before him under the same roof of their sprawling home. Shiva was just four years older than Vishnu, but his personality was calm and respectful. He was a gentleman with full of compassion whilst Vishnu was stubborn and self-serving. When Vishnu and Shiva both attended the village school, Shiva would do everything he could in his power to protect the other children from Vishnu’s attacks.
“Ramdev, your son disappoints me,” Ramdev’s father would say. “Gayatri has raised a tyrant. This is what happens when you marry someone for only their beauty and not from our caste. You can see so clearly now in Vishnu’s breeding. See how well brought up your brother’s children are. A mark of pedigree. Vishnu has to be set straight, otherwise he cannot be in the line of succession for village elder. He is a twisted boy, blinded by our wealth and position in my village. We have a responsibility to our people, Ramdev,” he paused before emphasizing, “A responsibility.”
Ramdev protested, assuring his father that Vishnu would change when the time came, but Ramdev’s father saw the evil in his grandson that Ramdev could not admit to. “No, he cannot stay here any longer,” the village elder silenced Ramdev’s justifications. “He will continue to sully our family’s name and we can no longer hide his inadequacies. He should get a taste for the real world. An old friend of ours owes us a favor. We will send your good-for-nothing son to live with him to Kochi. He will teach him what it takes to become a real man. He has no place here until he is straightened out. Enough now.”
No one could go against the village elder’s decisions. Not even his own son, Ramdev. Ramdev could see that there was no way around this and he had to submit to his father’s decision. His father was so powerful in the village council. He could sway the
other members to change the line of succession from Ramdev to his brother before his death.
“No”, thought Ramdev and stopped his protests. “Father is right, it is best that my boy moves to Kochi. All this evil eye around my handsome son. People are just jealous of him. I’ll let him go. At least he can enjoy his youth before getting stuck here like me. I was just like him when I was younger and I grew out of my old ways. Boys are just like that, but how can Father understand. He’s always loved my good-for-nothing brother and his wife and their three little imps. Look how Father is using my boy against me – he always wanted to pass on his lineage to his favorite son and is just trying to find an excuse to do so now. Just everyone wait and see. My son’s name is going to shine over everyone else’s when my son goes to Kochi – he’ll be a superstar. Then Father will want him to come back to parade him around the village. But by then, he’ll be king of his own fortune in Kochi. Just you see, you old, wicked man.”
~
Within a month of that conversation, arrangements were made to send Vishnu to Kochi to stay with the Iyer family. Kochi – the Queen of the Arabian Sea. Very few had ever left the village at least in the past century, so Vishnu’s departure was indeed a momentous occasion for the villagers no matter how disliked he was. Not many in the village had been to Kochi. Lakshmi’s father, and a handful of male folk, had visited there on very rare occasions in the past to deliver scrap metal or whatever needed to be sent there, but none could afford to stay long enough to enjoy its beauty and intrigue.
Arvind never did understand the allure of the big city and its chaos. He remembered smelling the salt in the seas, hearing the chatter of people and the cacophony of horns being blown
as traffic moved dangerously through the streets of the concrete jungle of vice and greed. He would tell them of all the unique aromas of cuisine that he could not even describe and the sacrilege practice of openly selling and eating meat. He admitted that he didn’t have the opportunity to really experience the true Kochi and didn’t expect to in this lifetime. One thing he did marvel over were the glimpses of luxury. There was such a variety of goods and sky-high buildings that he couldn’t even imagine how they could have been built.
But today, it was Vishnu’s moment as he headed out to Kochi. Unlike Arvind, Vishnu clearly had the means to be a young man there. The family said he was going to join a college program to study computers or business or something only wealthy families had access to. He was going to be an educated man. He was born to the class of the priests and the Gods bestowed such opportunities on people like him. The vast majority of villagers were lucky if they got to 10
th
class.
All the villagers gathered to bid Vishnu farewell and send him with him good wishes. He would travel in the family’s chauffeured car all the way to Kochi. Vishnu’s privileges were on full display that morning.
“Vah. The son of the village elder is going to Kochi. What isn’t possible when you’re born with a silver spoon in your mouth?” the villagers gossiped.
Lakshmi, her family and the other villagers all gathered in the town square to send Vishnu off. All huddled around the lanky pimply man with grease-slicked black hair and styled in western wear. Gayatri was wearing her best silk sari and holding the pooja plate to bless her one and only son ahead of his journey. She waved her hands over the flame of the diya and then placed her palm above his head, blessings him with good wishes of success for his new endeavors and a safe journey.
Vishnu was her pride and joy. Not wanting to cry in front of the whole village, she stood strong fighting back tears. Gayatri had practiced her stoicism over the years. She knew how to conceal her emotions, especially night after night of beatings and hearing how inadequate of a woman she had been. A woman from a lower class who had managed to climb her way into the highest echelon of society. No one knew the pain she endured. No one should ever know any woman’s pain in this village. She only knew that God blessed her at the right time, and despite her delicate condition, Vishnu triumphed in her womb.
And today, he had grown into this tall, handsome man of seventeen years standing so tall in front of her and all these villagers. Never seeing her son for what he really was, Gayatri stood in front of the entire village, proud that her son and not Shiva was getting this opportunity to make it big in the city. Then, she thought, his grandfather would see the extraordinary qualities of her son, who had for too long been trapped by the bounds of this small village.
Vishnu touched his grandfather’s feet, then his father’s, then his mother’s, then, Ravi’s, Shiva’s, Ram’s and Lakshman’s as he took each of his elders’ blessings before departing on his adventure.
“Kochi will be good for him. What fortune has been bestowed upon their boy and his family,” the village women chanted.
“He is young now, but when he comes back, he will as a man of the world. Then, we shall see who he will choose as his bride,” the eager women with daughters who would be of marriageable age by then, chirped.
~
Chapter 2 – Greed or Lobha
“Earth provides enough to satisfy every man's needs, but not every man's greed.”
~ Mahatma Gandhi
Vishnu did not return to the village until a couple of years. Through the chain of gossip from the Cook, to the women at the water well, to the women in the pick-up traveling up the hills of Idukki to the cardamom plantation, the villagers would discuss Vishnu’s great ventures in the big city.
“I hear that Vishnu has been doing well in Kochi. Not just well, but very well,” a big-nosed lady with a large chest tattled away. “He was a topper at the university he went to and has started in a top position at some multinational tech company in Kochi.”
Another added, “I heard he bought not just a car, but also a 3BHK along Marine Drive, overlooking the Arabian Sea.” Many of the villagers had never seen the sea or heard its waves crash against the shore. Somehow, however, they had heard all about Vishnu and the successful executive he had become in Kochi.
“We always knew he was bound for success,” the women said on the ride up to the estate one morning.
“He was bored in this small town and got up to all the tricks that boys do. He was destined for Kochi where he has become a great man,” another pencil thin woman explained.
“Ramdev and Gayatri are truly blessed from the Gods themselves. Wonder if he will return to Kochi to choose his wife or if he’ll find a city girl to match his ambitions,” a woman of a young marriageable daughter coyly inquired.
Lakshmi sat there at the back of the pick-up, the bumps in the road rocking her into a light slumber as she dreamt of being the great Vishnu’s Lakshmi just as in the Ramayan, the holy Hindu scriptures. She was lost in the endless possibilities that could take her from this tiny, boring village of limited opportunity to the big city of possibilities. Over cardamom tea, Kanika madam had told them all about the luxurious stores, trendy clothes, and the hustle and bustle of the big city.
Lakshmi dreamt of Vishnu’s return to the small village and him gallantly sweeping her off her feet and whisking her off to Kochi. She had grown into a young woman of fifteen, her breasts fully formed with complementing hips. As she walked around in the village, all the men feasted on her gorgeous figure and graceful features. Lakshmi prayed hard that her yearning to go to the city would be fulfilled by Goddess Lakshmi, and that when Vishnu came back to their small village, their family would court for her hand in marriage. It felt like a far-off dream as she also knew her place in society among the working class, but a girl could still dream.
Nevertheless, Lakshmi still held closely to this avenue of escape. Kanika would describe the Kochi streets bustling full of people, lit up by lights from morning to late at night, Lakshmi could hear the car horns, see the rickshaws whipsaw through the city, and see herself amongst the pedestrians from all levels of society converging into this magnificent city.
Lakshmi was young and not yet disillusioned, so she dared to dream. No one was being harmed by her doing so and if her dreams did indeed manifest into reality, then all the better. She would listen intently as Kanika madam would describe her shopping expeditions there and Lakshmi marveled at Kanika madam as she was clothed in the most beautiful, imported dresses her mother had bought for her from the top boutiques
lining Mahatma Gandhi Road in Kochi. For special occasions like weddings or Hindu celebrations, Kanika madam would show Lakshmi and the other girls beautifully embroidered saris or Indian dresswear from the top designers.
Lakshmi couldn’t ever remember the name of the boutiques but was in rapture when Kanika madam brought out the delicate fabrics in the whole spectrum of colors. Lakshmi had worn the same salwar kameez outfit every day for the past who knows how long. She had kept exceptional care of the few pairs of clothes she owned and the one pair of shoes she had. Clothes and shoes were meticulously mended so that they could last the longest stretch possible, until nothing could save them from being tossed and replaced.
Lakshmi craved a life like Kanika madam’s and the chance to escape the constant routine she was born into. Her family and her were just better than the untouchables, so at least the upper crust of society didn’t feel disgust when she was in their presence. Yet, Lakshmi wanted more than that life. She wanted a life of privilege, to adorn herself in the latest fashions and mingle with the ritzy socialites in Kochi. She wanted a life like Kanika madam’s.
One morning on the pick-up trust, Lakshmi heard that Vishnu would finally be returning to the village soon. The rumor mill at the well was also turned up on high volume and over the course of a few days, Lakshmi learned that Vishnu was not just coming home, but coming home to find himself a wife to take back to Kochi. Lakshmi knew that if given the chance, she could do more with her life than just repeating the life of her mother in this small village of monotony. The same routine every day suited Devi. She had never sought growth of any kind and contently performed the same duties like clockwork ever since she married Arvind. She took pride in wearing the exact same outfits from
day to day, eating the same bowl of rice twice a day, and working her hands raw at the cardamom estate.
“How does one get out of this situation if one cannot escape this small village?” Lakshmi thought to herself as her eyes lingered across the tranquil scenery as the pick-up drove on. The trees were rustling in the gentle wind and bowed to its southwesterly directly as the sun peeked its golden rays, touching the lichen covering tree bark and the surfaces of rocks.
“Lakshmi, eh, Lakshmi. We’re here. Time to get out of the pick-up. Silly girl. Where is your mind these days? Come, we have to get out of the truck now and into work, madam.” her mother said as she shook her daughter awake.
And snap, Lakshmi was back into her real world. As they walked into the farm, Lakshmi took all the energy she could muster, blocked everything out from her surroundings, and prayed as hard as she could to Goddess Laksmi.
“Please Lakshmi Mata. Please save me from this dull and tedious existence. I know in my heart that I am meant for a greater destiny. The whole village is always speaking of my beauty and the good fortune I brought my family. Vishnu is coming back to the village. May he choose me to be his wife,” Lakshmi prayed deeper than she had ever done before and felt the presence of Goddess Lakshmi in that moment, until she was interrupted.
“Lakshmi, Lakshmi. Your head is in the clouds again. Wake up and hand me the basket,” her annoyed mother interceded as she pulled Lakshmi out of her thoughts and into her work.
~
One morning, there happened to be a lot of commotion at the grandest house in the village. Even before the first birds started chirping, everyone in the household was up and about. The
village elder, Ramdev, Gayatri, his brother, his brother’s wife, Shiva, Shiva’s wife, Shiva’s daughter, and the two newly-wed twin brothers with their brides were up and about. The house was full and preparations underway for Vishnu’s arrival from Kochi. No one but Ramdev had ever traveled the distance to Kochi to see how his son had been doing. Ramdev would come home gloating to the family of Vishnu’s successful executive position and large 3BHK overlooking the Arabian Sea. Ramdev was more than happy to rub all his son’s successes in their faces, especially in his father’s face.
“You were right to have my only son go to Kochi, father. I owe you that. But now we have lost him forever to that city of opportunities. He cannot come back here to take some menial role at this small village. You were right. My boy could not fill your shoes; they are far too small for him. You were right all along, father. Shiva is the right successor here,” Ramdev said, as strutted shamelessly in front of the village elder.
That morning, the Tulsi plant was watered with the first water from the well and prayers sang to usher in a peaceful and joyous return of Ramdev and Gayatri’s only son and pride. Today, Ramdev was feeling especially happy. He drank not one but two glasses of saffron-infused sweetened milk. In honor of his son’s return, the villagers had been invited to gather in the village center to get a glimpse of the young man, now completely transformed and the most eligible bachelor of the village. Vishnu could have the pick of any woman in the village. Ramdev and Gayatri felt bless to be the envy of everyone.
In the kitchen, Cook, under the strict supervision of Gayatri, was packaging the payasam that she prepared very early that morning. The sweet, nutty milk dessert would be distributed to every family in the village to commemorate Vishnu’s return from his adventures in Kochi. For certain packages an invitation was
included to invite eligible young women to a small gathering at the grand house to meet the family and of course, the prospective groom. One of these packages was to be sent to Lakshmi’s house.
Even though Lakshmi came from a class much, much below theirs, Lakshmi’s effortless beauty was the talk of the town. A fair maiden mistakenly placed by the Gods in the house of laborers as a mischievous playful act. Gayatri had consulted the village mystic who had been her confidant during her many misgivings over the years, and the village mystic had provided Gaytri with assurances that an invitation to such a household was not only fine, but in fact, would be the most auspicious match for Vishnu. Given the alignment of the stars on each of their birth charts, such a wife would only increase Vishnu’s good fortunes by ten-folds. With that consent and assurance in hand, Gayatri was determined to have her son marry the most sought-after girl this village had to offer, since, perhaps Gayatri herself. Gayatri was determined to maneuver her son’s affections towards Lakshmi.
~
The villagers gathered in the village center as they saw a motorcade slowly approach. They first saw a pick-up adorned with a garland, which was then followed by a trio of men dressed in marching band attire with one playing drums, another a trumpet, and the last on a clarinet. They blasted tunes, welcoming home the prodigal son of the highest-ranking family in the village. As they watched, the villagers craned their necks but he did not seem to appear at the back of the pick-up or even up front with its driver. Finally, they saw a brand-new Hero Honda car trailing behind the pick-up truck and the band. The villagers’ eyes widened as they gawked at that sleek vehicle as it
came rolling down the rough village road. A group of little boys caught up with the car and ran beside it, pretending to push it forward with their little hands.
“Vah! Vah! How fabulous! What new fortune Vishnu has brought to our small village,” the villagers stared, wide-eyed, as they marveled at the car rolling by. They had only seen such a classy ride in a newspaper ad or on one of those billboard ads painted on the wall of the auto shop.
