They tried to peek in, but the windows were tinted and they could only make out a silhouette of a man in the driver’s seat.
“That Vishnu was always a risk-taker and look at the wealthy he’s amassed in Kochi,” they sang in unison, knowing that it could only be Vishnu behind that wheel.
The car came to a stop and Vishnu stepped out in a tailored black suit and long purple tie, with matching shoes and an expensive looking watch. He donned washed and neatly set hair that was slicked back with a heavy dollop of gel. He looked like a hero from one of those Tollywood movies. The Allu Arjun of their village. They could see Vishnu’s face plastered on one of those huge billboards ads for Hero Honda painted.
Ramdev and Gayatri proudly met their son in front of the large crowd gathered around, equally impressed and relishing in the envy surrounding them. Gayatri was decked out in one of her fine silk saris and bejeweled from head to town. She had her pooja plate front and center as she blessed her son home, thanking the Gods for his safe return. Her hand swept over the flame of the diya, which was burning on the plate and she ran her warm palm over her son’s head in three successive motions. She then broke a piece of coconut burfi to sweeten her son’s mouth after his tiresome journey to praise his return and to usher in the next dawn of good luck and fortune. Vishnu touched his grandfather’s feet, then his father’s, then his mother’s, his
uncle’s, Shiva’s, and then Ram’s and Lakhan’s out of respect and for their blessings.
The music began and the village folk (except the uninvited untouchables) enjoyed the feast arranged for them to celebrate Vishnu’s arrival. They marveled at the luxurious spread from idli, dosa, sambar, coconut chutney and many more delicacies most of the villagers would only ever have on very special occasions once or maybe twice a year. Today was certainly one of those auspicious moments. It was more fun than the villagers had had in a long time, and was almost as big as the annual fair the village would host, but just without the races, musical numbers, dance competition and other activities.
Around the middle of the mini-festival, the village elder ushered everyone to gather. He first thanked everyone for taking part in the day to celebrate the return of his grandson, even though his grandson was there only for a short leave from his busy new life in Kochi. He went on to explain how today was a very important day, which had been selected by the village mystic. It was important because it had been specially chosen to announce their intent to invite selected young women from eligible families to their grand house in the following days so that Vishnu could select his future wife who would follow him to Kochi.
“Wow! This is unbelievable,” the villagers chanted.
“It is about time. He needs a wife to manage his house and wealth,” a woman inserted into the gossip that buzzed through the villagers.
“I wonder who will be selected. Regardless, she will be a very lucky woman. Her fate will be tied to the family and their riches,” another added.
One by one, the village elder summoned the eligible young women’s father or head of household and handed the payasam with the invitation.
“Father of Lakshmi,” the village elder spoke.
Silence swept across the villagers, who were crowded around the village elder.
Stunned for a brief moment, Devi looked blankly at the faces around her. “Was she hearing that right?” Lakshmi’s mother thought to herself.
“Father of Lakshmi. Mother of Lakshmi. Where are you?” the village elder repeated irritated by the lack of response.
As Arvind was away on one of his jobs, a dumbfounded Devi walked up to him and graciously took the payasam and invite, containing her utter excitement. “This is really happening. Really happening. I always knew my girl was special,” Lakshmi’s mother thought, forcibly concealing a smile and her pride. “How our footing in this village will change with such an opportunistic alliance if our Lakshmi is selected.”
She straightened her back as she walked back to her place in the crowd and then unable to conceal her joy any longer, she let out a smug smile. All of a sudden, Devi stood so erect. She was filled with new found confidence and pride that could be felt by every other woman around her. She basked in their jealousy.
~
Devi invited Trisha, her best friend, over to the house for her advice. Trisha insisted she would be of little use, but Devi enticed her with the promise of Lakshmi’s revered cardamom tea. Once the two women were together, Trisha began providing unsolicited advice with Devi intently listening, “Devi. Now listen
carefully. Don’t take this opportunity for granted just because your daughter is simply stunning. It’s not every day an invitation like this comes to a home like ours. You will have to teach your girl a few maneuvers to charm the fellow. The tongues of those women from the upper class are sharp and will take every opportunity to muddy her reputation if things are not handled properly.”
Devi and Trisha had known each other since they were both young girls themselves and had grown up only a few houses apart from one another. They had celebrated the good times and suffered through the hardest of times together. They were always there for one another.
Trisha had secretly eyed Lakshmi for her oldest son, Krishna, but she never had the courage to ask Devi for Lakshmi’s hand given their sisterly relationship. Plus, Krishna was stocky and of a smaller stature. He was dusky in color and already had thinning hair as a young man of nineteen that no amount of coconut oil helped save.
Devi had always been arrogant and protective over her precious Lakshmi and boasted how Lakshmi was destined to marry someone handsome, tall, and of course, fair like her. Vishnu, being as rich as he was, would be the icing on the cake. Trisha was pleased for her friend having received the invitation. The prospect of her best friend’s daughter not being chosen, however, seemed certain to her. Trisha had surmised that surely the wealthiest family of their village wasn’t going to select the lowly village girl, no matter how beautiful she was. They had a certain class and status to maintain. She was sure that the other mothers from the priest class wouldn’t allow this and would do anything to sabotage the match.
If Trisha was right, and she felt very good about the odds, she knew that Devi’s smugness would have to quickly melt away
with all the shame she would face from the rejection. It would be at that time that Trisha would victoriously step in and offer to help her friend. At that time, Trisha thought, she would ask for Lakshmi’s hand in marriage for her Krishna. Sure, Krishna wasn’t tall, fair, handsome or even rich, but he was more than a disgraced Lakshmi would be worth then.
Devi would be at her lowest and desperate. “Then”, Trisha thought as Devi was muttering away about the preparations, “she will agree and we will see who has the upper hand. I’ll make sure that my Krishna will have the fairest bride and I, a daughter-in-law that will be so grateful for my kindness. Finally, I will have someone to take the burden of my household chores from me and make me special cardamom tea when I ask for it.”
~
Lakshmi’s grandmother was quietly lying on the cot listening to Devi and Trisha as they strategically came up with a plan on how Lakshmi would capture the attention of Vishnu and his mother, Gayatri during their “interview”. Lakshmi’s grandmother knew better than to meddle in the affairs of Devi. In the past, she had tried to encourage Devi to think more about her daughter’s future in broader terms than just having her married off. Lakshmi was smart and curious to learn. Her grandmother saw the great scholar Lakshmi could be.
It was pointless. Devi saw an independent woman in their society as trouble. That was garbage propaganda that the Tollywood movies tried to push. It only worked for wealthy women. Not village folk like them.
Lakshmi’s grandmother was a progressive woman, trapped in this bubble. In her old age, she had become dependent on Devi and her son, having no resources for herself. She had tried to
be a staunch advocate for Lakshmi to read more, draw, and listen to current events on the transistor radio to broaden her perspectives. She would sneak books to her granddaughter at night after she got home from the Idukki Eco Farm that she had found thrown away or had borrowed from the old shopkeeper friend of hers. She encouraged her granddaughter to learn more than what was within the walls of their daily village life.
“Why do you have her waste her time listening to that nonsense on the radio? We don’t live in a big city or in foreign. Who cares what is happening in Amrika? Put some music on. It’s been such a long day. I want to relax now.” Devi would argue with her mother-in-law.
On other days, Devi would be heard yelling at Lakshmi’s grandmother, “She is too beautiful and will must be married young. She should focus on learning to take care of the household and not on these things. Come Lakshmi, let me show you how to mend your dad’s shirt.”
After some time, Lakshmi’s grandmother grew tired of the back-and-forth bickering. To maintain peace in the household, she decided it was best that she stayed quiet and in the background. As she grew older, she lost the fight to advocate for her granddaughter’s future. She hoped and prayed that Lakshmi would live a different life than hers or Devi’s. A life of independence and freedom.
Perhaps there was no escape from the role of a dutiful wife? Perhaps education was only for men and for the women of upper classes? Her fighting spirit had finally been beaten out of her after eighty decades. Her words had always been met by abject resistance, but the prospect of this marriage brought possibilities and hope. “If Lakshmi can go to the big city, she would have better opportunities than she would ever have here,” Lakshmi’s grandmother prayed for the marriage’s fruition, but
deep down in her gut, she was hesitant knowing Vishnu and his past. “Maybe he truly has changed as they are saying? He was a boy when he left here, and returned a man,” Lakshmi’s grandmother thought as she eavesdropped on Devi and Trisha.
~
“You must spend some money, Devi, and get a few high-class items for your daughter to wear for the big day. Make sure she starts applying the turmeric mask to that face so she glows even fairer and brighter the day of. And put a touch of rouge on her cheeks and stain her lips. It is best if you can clean up her fingernails too and get any dirt off of them. And apply some coconut oil to them to make them feel softer. Don’t let her do too much work around the house and make sure she gets plenty of rest,” Trisha instructed as she took charge of Lakshmi. Despite her secret prediction, Trisha was a well-intentioned woman. Well, she was very confident that Lakshmi would be rejected. She figured that if she was kind now, she would have even stronger leverage when the time came to ask for Lakshmi’s hand in marriage for her son.
Devi had always looked up to Trisha, even though Trisha was only a few months older than her. Trisha moved with a certain confidence and grace that Devi always felt she never had. They were polar opposites in physique. Trisha was shorter, darker, less attractive, but she always seemed to have command over every aspect of life. It was Trisha that helped Devi through her miscarriages as Trisha easily popped out her sons with ease. It was Trisha that told her to consult the village mystic and who had accompanied Devi on all the rituals that ultimately helped her conceive Lakshmi.
Without Trisha, Devi would have been so lost. The gossip and taunts of her infertility would have crushed her. Only Trisha was
ever there for her. Devi’s mother-in-law talked about woman independence and progress, but when the time came, she shunned Devi and advised her son to look for a new, younger wife to bear the next generation. Trisha, on the other hand, never judged. She never told her she was inadequate or weak. She would cheer her up even on days when Devi found it hard to get herself out of bed. She was there. And Trisha was here now again to help Devi and Devi’s daughter. Devi was grateful for having such a true and everlasting friendship.
“And what about that silk sari. It is a little old but they don’t make them that way anymore. The lilac color will complement her fair skin and almond eyes. It will be a subtle touch, just enough to make her pop in front of those rich girls, but not too much to make her look too desperate.” Trisha continued on.
Devi continued listening, taking thorough notes in her head as she admired her friend’s endless wisdom.
“And if that day she makes her special cardamom tea, well that should seal the deal. Those silly rich girls don’t know their way around the kitchen. They don’t even know the difference between cardamom and cloves. Vishnu will need a wife that can do some basic household duties. I’m sure in the big city they’ll have a maid, but he will want to be reminded of his village from time to time. Of his mother’s cooking. He came back home for a wife after all,” Trisha explained with such logic and clarity. Devi marveled at her friend’s wisdom.
And into the night the two ladies continued on, preparing for Lakshmi’s first meeting with Vishnu and the wealthiest family in their small village on the outskirts of the Idukki hills.
~
The days leading up to the meeting with Vishnu and his family
were agonizing for Lakshmi. She felt every emotion imaginable. At times, she felt super excited to have been chosen and the only girl from a lower class. Other times, she felt completely nervous and anxious. If she wasn’t chosen, then she knew that she would bring shame to her family. The family would have to suffer taunts from the other women. That pressure was agonizing as she knew what her mother had gone through before her birth.
“She may be beautiful, but they should have considered their position in society before accepting such an invitation,” she imagined that the villagers would chatter behind their backs but for the less shameless, straight to Devi’s face.
“They just wanted to have some sport. For these rich people, we are just there for humiliation. They should have known better, but Devi is so opportunistic,” she ruminated what others could say.
Lakshmi couldn’t bear to hear anything against her family and wondered if accepting this invitation was the right step to take. Yet, at the same time, Vishnu would be her only way out of this small village. An opportunity like this would likely never come again.
She spent the late nights reading the books her grandmother had secretly snuck to her. She even got a Malory Towers book from Kanika once. She had read that book over and over again. There were so many exciting prospects outside this tiny world she had always known. She knew that there was a life for her beyond picking the right cardamom pods at the exact time they were ready to be harvested. That was a life her mother was good with, but she dared to dream bigger. She was like her grandmother and wanted more for herself. She may have been born into a poor family, may have had her formal education cut short, but Lakshmi did not have to accept this life forever. She didn’t want to end up like her mother. She didn’t want to be yet another
generation of women stuck in this small village with limited opportunity. She saw it clearly now. Vishnu was her ticket out of here.
~
The day before the big “interview”, Lakshmi made a conscience effort to drown out the gossiping voices of the women as they rode up in the pick-up to the cardamom estate. The women congratulated Devi and her daughter on the invite. Secretly, however, they were all thinking along the same lines as Trisha. Despite Lakshmi’s God-given beauty, she still wouldn’t win the hearts of the wealthy because of her family’s low social status.
That didn’t hold some of them back from expressing this concern. “No amount of beauty will make up for the fact that she was born into the labor class, Devi,” one woman chimed in, despite the stares from the other women trying to urge her to remain quite through some form of ESP.
“Shush. You’re all just jealous that your daughters weren’t chosen. Gayatri was once one of us, remember. And now see how she has fared,” Trisha slammed back, vehemently protecting her friend and her daughter. Although Trisha was convinced otherwise, she put on an air of support for her beloved friend. She believed this strategy would work in her favor when Lakshmi was rejected. Devi would come groveling to and pleading Trisha to take Lakshmi as her daughter-in-law.
Lakshmi half-listened to the women’s chatter. Tomorrow was going to be the day that would decide her future. The day when her fortune could change in a blink of an eye, in or out of her favor. At times, she felt terribly nervous. She tried to ground herself in reality, reminding herself that she could not change the result and that Goddess Lakshmi had already determined
her fate. She could only do her best to present herself as the best possible candidate for Vishnu’s wife. Earlier that morning, she had spent some meditative moments in the Gangalee fields as she did every morning after doing her chores. It hadn’t been peaceful as her mind raced between the different possibilities of the interview results. She was annoyed at her agitation and her inability to focus on her drawings or anything else.
Now at the back of the pick-up, she started feeling suffocated with the same restlessness. She took in deep breaths, inhaling the crisp, dewy morning air of the Idukki hills and quietly exhaling as not to draw attention to her nerves from the scavenging women upfront. As she sat in the corner of the pick-up bed, she begged the trees with their leaves dressed in the morning dew to give her some form of peace. She pleaded with the little creatures that scurried through the flora to distract her today.
Regardless of the result tomorrow, her whole life would change direction for better or worse. Everything she knew before would cease to exist as it was. She beckoned for courage that if she was chosen that she would be the perfect bride. The perfect wife. That she would bear the most beautiful sons. That she would put her family name on the map and it would shine so bright that no one would ever dare to look down on her family again. She tried to dispel the negative thoughts of the shame and despair that would befall her family if she was rejected.
Focusing on achieving the match, Lakshmi realized that this would also mean that today would be the last tea party she would share with Kanika madam and the other young girls. She would miss gathering around the fancy fine bone China teapot from England that Aaya would pour the freshly brewed cardamom tea from and into the Made in India steel cups for the girls to
enjoy. It would be the last time she would have those buttery tea biscuits.
As she thought of the past tea parties, she then smiled to herself. If she were to succeed, then perhaps she too would be sitting in a pretty chiffon dress with her hair tied perfectly back in a neat ponytail like Kanika madam. With Vishnu’s wealth, she would be a madam of the house and she too could have her own Mamta serving tea to underprivileged girls. Those girls too would then have the chance to dream of one day becoming like her, a wealthy benefactor. It was such a beautiful dream, which was suddenly cut short by her mother yelling at her to come out of her daydream and get out of the pick-up truck.
“Silly girl. You haven’t received the proposal yet. Today, you still have to work,” Devi said, gently smacking the back of Lakshmi’s neck with her palm. Devi did this more as an act of discipline in front of the other ladies as she could feel her own cockiness and pride. Devi had been riding high after the news, but anxiety was starting to get the better of her. “What if Lakshmi isn’t chosen?” Devi thought. “The shame. The shame.” She shook her head as they clocked in to begin their shift. Devi poured in all her effort into the work to distract herself from the racing thoughts that oscillated between Lakshmi’s proposal being a success versus a flop.
~
As they sat down to afternoon tea with Kanika madam, Lakshmi’s mind wandered off again as she held her steel cup of steaming hot cardamom tea in her small, tired hands.
All of a sudden, her thoughts were interrupted as she heard the other girls telling Kanika madam that Lakshmi had received
a prospective marriage invitation from the village elder’s household.
“Kanika madam. Do you know our very own, Lakshmi, is seeing a prospective groom tomorrow?” one of the older girls with tightly bound pig-tails on either side of her head shared.
“He is from the wealthiest family in our village and is now some big, hot shot businessman in Kochi. Maybe, once Lakshmi is there, you may see her on one of your trips to Kochi, Kanika madam,” another girl mentioned.
“Wow, that is wonderful Lakshmi. Congratulations! I could never imagine getting married so…so young, but you, village girls always do,” Kanika madam chuckled. “I don’t think I would want to get married until I’m at least in my mid-20s. I still have to go to university, get my MBA, and see New York, London Paris, Singapore,” she went on. The attention had shifted off of Lakshmi and back to Kanika madam in that moment.
The village girls had no idea what an MBA was or all those places Kanika madam had just rattled off, but it sounded fancy to all of them. They marveled at the luxury of time that rich women had in choosing when to get married. A rich person’s privilege! They could do whatever they wanted. Why, if they chose not to get married like Kanika madam’s aunt, these wealthy heiresses could do so with no consequences or shame. Kanika madam’s aunt was considered a fascinating recluse, full of allure and intrigue. If she lived in their village, she would be a laughingstock and called a childless, old cow that had been too picky with her prospects and now living off of the handouts from her burdened family. She would be a pariah.
“Are you excited, Lakshmi?” Kanika madam shifted the attention back at Lakhsmi, noticing that Lakshmi had left her
biscuit soaking in her cardamom tea too long that the poor biscuit had gotten too and fallen into the depths of the hot tea.
“Huh?! Sorry, Kanika madam, what was that?” Lakshmi replied, snapping back into reality.
The girls erupted in laughter.
“Looks like Lakshmi is day-dreaming again,” the girls teased.
A bewildered Lakshmi smiled a lovely, good-natured smile with her pearly white teeth peeking through her naturally rosy lips. The young women giggled together like schoolgirls chatting over a boy crush. Lakshmi blushed, exposing her dimples and bashfully hid behind her best friend, Rani. Luckily, the attention moved away from Lakshmi and back to usual topics like the latest fashion trends Kanika madam had seen on her last trip to Kochi, the hot gossip of the Tollywood’s actors and actresses, and the latest steamy romance novel Kanika madam had thumbed through recently.
~
That night, Rani stayed over at Lakshmi’s house, where she helped Devi and Trisha arrange Lakshmi’s outfit including jewelry and make-up for the next morning. Lakshmi had been applying turmeric paste every evening to her face and was told to do very light work that kept her away from the tanning rays of the sun. God forbid her skin darkened and emphasized the peasant class she had been born into. Rani had helped out with the household chores and fetched water from the well over the last few days. Devi and Trisha fawned over Lakshmi and were now engaged in a squabble over what sari Lakshmi should wear, despite this having been agreed on before.
“Devi, listen. Lilac will accentuate her fine features and complement her skin tone,” Trisha urged.
“Lakshmi, come here…Come. Come here, my child. Try on this pink sari. It is a more traditional color. No, this red one is better,” Lakshmi’s mother lashed back at her friend as she tugged at her daughter towards her, pulling her away from Trisha’s grasp.
The two women went back and forth for around a half hour until her grandmother finally could take more of the bickering, “I have to agree with Trisha on this, Devi. The lilac sari really makes Lakshmi’s features stand out. You don’t want her to just be another traditional girl. Vishnu is from the big city. He’s looking for a bit of modern and traditional mix.”
“See, Devi. Even your mother-in-law has more sense than you. And let’s tie it this way so her midriff shows just enough to tempt Vishnu but at the same time protect her dignity and the family honor. It is a tricky balance, especially for us folks,” Trisha explained and gesturing an okay sign, pleased at her knowledge of these nuances.
As usual, Devi folded to her friend’s wisdom despite feeling so strongly about the sari. She listened intently as if Trisha was so knowledgeable and worldly. She felt this way even though their upbringings were similar and they were just a few months apart in age. It had always been that way. Devi hardly ever made a decision of her own and looked to Trisha for direction. She held her friend and her advice on the highest pedestal. Even so, no one could argue that Trisha was a force to reckon with. She had always been by Devi’s side, in both happiness and sadness. That was more love and support she had received from anyone, even from her own mother or her husband, Arvind.
As the evening went on, the women continued to make preparations for the “interview”. They went through the meager jewelry possessions that they owned between the two of them and styled the perfect outfit. Trisha had even asked Krishna
to run to the market early the next morning to get a gajra to adorn Lakshmi’s hair with the sweet scent of jasmine flowers and to further accent her carefully coordinated look. They had massaged warm coconut oil into Lakshmi’s hair that would be washed out the next morning to give her hair that extra shine and bounce. Her eyebrows were naturally arched and Trisha ever so gently threaded them just a bit further to bring a bit more sophistication and definition to them. Between them, they chose the nicest looking set of slippers that would just peek out from under the lilac sari and Trisha, being the more cultured of the two, showed Lakshmi how to walk so her gait remained elegant and the attention was never on the worn-out slippers.
Over the past few days, they had overwhelmed Lakshmi with advice on how to politely and playfully charm Vishnu and his family, distracting from the poverty that she so clearly came from. Fortunately, Lakshmi was naturally charismatic.
“Now hurry, Lakshmi. You need to get to rest. Rani will paint your nails that light pink purple shade and then off to bed the two of you,” the women beckoned as they disappeared into the kitchen to chit chat some more over some cardamom tea.
~
Rani and Lakshmi had grown up together in the small village nestled at the foot of the Idukki hills. They both had very similar stories just like Trisha and Devi, and the many young girls of the village had. At some point their parents had to make the not-so-difficult decision to pull them out of school and put them to work on the local farms. After all, their only purpose in life was to get married young and serve as wives and mothers. The villagers, led by the women, always pondered, “What would an education possibly do for girls like them? What good had it done for any woman from their village?”
The girls were roughly the same age, being only a few days apart. Rani was the oldest daughter with three brothers and a sister, all of whom had come much later in birth than her. She had more or less taken the role of a mother for all of them after their own mother had died in childbirth, giving birth to her little sister. Her mother had named her Rani, hoping that one day she would be the queen of her own kingdom and escape the squalor of this village. Like Lakshmi, she started work at the cardamom estate at a ripe age and would hop on a slightly later ride than Lakshmi after completing her chores and sending her siblings off to school. The owners of the Idukki Eco Lodge were thoughtful and provided leniency, having known Rani’s mother prior to her tragic passing.
Despite the obstacles, young Rani was hopeful and idealistic. She found ways to distract from the hurt. When Rani did the household chores and ran after her siblings, she would pretend she was a wealthy heiress playing make believe. It had been just her and her siblings at home most days. Her father went on long trucking jobs and spent most of the little time he was home, drunk and worn after spending the little money he earned on booze and the local brothel. Her maternal aunt and uncle lived in a village nearby and would visit every big festival or when they could. They doted on Rani and helped her out whenever they could. They vowed that one day they would find her a suitable raja to rescue her from the life she led just as her mother had wishes.
Rani was a unique soul. Despite losing her mother at such a young age and having to take care of the brood left behind, she shouldered those responsibilities with such positivity that no one bothered to see the terrible tragedy she bore.
“Lakshmi, how lucky you are,” Rani said, concealing her jealousy, as she painted the first coat of polish on Lakshmi’s
almond-shaped fingernails. “You truly are a rose that has grown among us thorns, and I have no doubt that Vishnu will choose you, tomorrow.” She said this with some pain as she beamed with a forced smile.
“I sure hope so, Rani. I cannot wait to leave this village and experience the wonders of the big city. Marrying Vishnu will open that door for sure. It will be like a romance in one of those Mills & Boon novels that Kanika madam reads to us. Vishnu, my dashing savior, will sweep me off my feet and treat me like a queen in his luxury 3BHK. And once I settle into life in Kochi, I will find a raja for you too, Rani. One of his industrialist friends, perhaps?! And we will both live like queens that we were born to be,” Lakshmi responded light-heartedly as she quashed the intense nervousness.
Both girls sat there, delighting in the prospect of both leaving their mundane life, which was in itself a kind of servitude handed down to them from generations of yore. They couldn’t wait to be the two girls to break this vicious cycle. And it just made sense that Lakshmi would be able to do so first.
She was the prettier of the two and had always been. She was fair-skinned, tall and had the thickest and darkest hair that would cascade down her back. Rani was also fair, but just not quite as fair as Lakshmi. She was more blessed in her chest area, but her boyish hips added to her stockier figure. Her hair was very curly and because she just didn’t have the luxury of time with needing to care for and feed four younger siblings and a transient drunk father, she kept it cropped adding to her boyishness. When they walked the village streets, she felt invisible beside Lakshmi and certainly not the one that the village men would ogle at when they went to the water wells in the morning.
Rani finally finished the last coat of paint and Lakshmi’s tired hands all of a sudden brightened with the subtle pink purple shade. They looked so elegant as bit by bit she transformed into a true contender for tomorrow’s contest over the most eligible bachelor of the wealthiest family in their small village on the cusp of the Idukki hills.
The two girls talked some more about how they would both get married and move to the big city. They would be neighbors swimming in luxury, never having to worry about fetching water from the well or even cooking the meager batches of rice ever again. Everything would be plentiful.
They lay together on Lakshmi’s bed in a room Lakshmi shared with her mother and grandmother. They spent a while longer chatting away about what intimacy with a man would be like, bringing alive the passages Kanika madam had read to them over afternoon tea. They imagined the gentle touch of a man’s lips on their soft skin and how the slow kisses would arouse sensations they had never spoke openly about as they matured. They wondered how their first night with their respective husbands would be like and if it would be as full of passion as described in those Mills & Boon novels. The girls giggled as they had these unholy thoughts, but their chatter was cut short when Trisha and Devi scolded them and told them to go to sleep.
~
The morning of the big day had finally arrived. The day that Lakshmi would be paraded in front of Vishnu and his family and they would decide whether she was of marriageable material into the high priest caste. Diligently, Rani continued on covering Lakshmi’s chores. Today, the household’s full attention was on ensuring the day went smoothly for Lakshmi and the family’s future.
Rani brought in the water from the well and Devi put it on the fire to heat it up so it was just warm enough for Lakshmi to bathe with. As she splashed the bath water over her body, Devi put a few drops of oil that she had infused with the petals of the sweet smelling Gangalee flower. Like a true queen, Lakshmi bathed in the water gently scented by the floral oil as left its perfume on her body.
Devi thoroughly washed out the coconut oil in her daughter’s hair, leaving Lakshmi’s locks bouncy and silky. After the bath, Devi put a few drops of the Gangalee oil in Lakshmi’s hair careful not to make it greasy again, but to add a hint reminiscent of the Gangalee fields of the village. Trisha had recommended this touch, which would subtly remind Vishnu of his hometown. She also put a bit on Lakshmi’s wrists, cleavage and on her neck. Trisha and Devi then spent the next half hour fussing over how to wrap the sari to accentuate Lakshmi’s figure and at the same time revealing just enough of Lakshmi’s sculpted midriff to tempt Vishnu.
As Lakshmi’s hair air dried, Devi applied kajol to Lakshmi’s eyeline, accentuating her already stunning almond-shaped eyes. Her fair skin was so smooth and unblemished that there was not need to apply any concealer or powder make-up. Devi applied a delicate dusting of blush to Lakshmi’s cheeks and just a little on the tip of her button nose. And with just every little bit of adornment, Lakshmi’s beauty shone more beautiful than it had ever before.
“I’m thinking to not add the gold chain. Just earrings should be enough? And no lipstick? She already has such delicate rose-colored lips.” Trisha questioned.
“Hahn. You are right, Trisha. The silver tear drop earrings are more than enough and let’s add a simple red bindi and these lilac bangles,” Devi dutifully responded. Devi was so proud of
her daughter and so thankful to Goddess Lakhsmi for having bestowed such a gift after years of difficulty.
Even though Rani’s hair was cropped, she was the best out of them at styling hair after having years of practice taking care of her little sister’s and in her spare time, she would play with Lakshmi’s. Rani worked her magic creating a thick, cascading waterfall braid. As directed, Krishna had arrived with a fresh gajra and just in time for Rani to add to her masterpiece.
“Well done my boy! Right on time!” Trisha sang her son’s praises. “He brings you luck with his auspicious timing.” Trisha beamed as she dropped a good word in for her son in anticipation of Lakshmi’s rejection later that day.
And with that addition, Lakshmi’s look was complete. She stood up in front of an old cracked full-size mirror that hang on one side of the shared wardrobe’s door. She was shocked at her transformation from an innocent peasant girl into a gorgeous young woman, ready for suitors and even high society. As she marveled in her glow-up, she couldn’t help but feel even more confident and self-assured that this was her moment to shine. Lakshmi now felt she truly had a fighting chance of beating all the other candidates.
Devi, Trisha, and Rani circled her, admiring Lakshmi and dumbfounded as to how something so beautiful could grace the likes of them. They called out to Lakshmi’s grandmother who hobbled in on her cane.
“Wow!” Lakshmi’s grandmother exclaimed, “If I didn’t know it, I would think the Queen of England was in front of us.” They all laughed and Lakshmi’s grandmother came up to her and put a black mark at the back of Lakshmi’s ear to ward off any evil eye and with that, Lakshmi was ready to step outside to show herself to the villagers who had gathered by their house.
~
Lakshmi elegantly stepped out of her house donned in her fine silks as if she was born into the lifestyle of the rajas and ranis. She saw their family friends, relatives, and other villagers lined up in the street to see her and her parents off. Lakshmi now stood in stark contrast to their ragged clothes and dull, sun beaten complexions. They had come out to show her their support, at least that’s what it seemed from the outside. For some villagers, jealous thoughts had found a place and then, there was always that clique of women that never really liked Trisha and Devi and seethed in their ill will for them.
“Look how they’ve glammed her up shamelessly thinking that someone from our class has a chance in the priest class family,” they muttered amongst themselves.
“After he rejects her, that will knock some sense into them. That Devi always turned her nose up at our sons. They were never good enough for her daughter. But just wait when Devi comes back down from the clouds,” a middle-aged mother of two good-for-nothing sons grumbled.
“Ha. The beauty is only there for one or two days. We need a girl that isn’t a decoration piece, but someone who can run a household, bear some healthy sons, and above all take care of her husband’s needs,” another woman said spitefully.
With her head held high, Lakshmi walked past the well-wishers and the not-so-well-wishers. She walked with a new confidence brought on by the delicate make-up illuminating her God-given features and sophisticated dress. She felt like a Tollywood actress, all dolled up with adoring fans watching her with envy and lust as she walked down a red carpet for her latest movie premier.
All the nerves she felt over the past few days faded away. She was so sure in that moment that she would charm Vishnu and her family today. She was more than just beautiful. She was charming and eloquent. Plus, she knew how to cook, clean, and had her fair share of taking care of her younger sibling and cousin. She was respectful to her elders and was always amicable.
She imagined that she had so much more to offer than the other candidates. Those women may hail from the priest class, but they couldn’t match her in beauty or wit. Unlike them, she also was hungry to leave her place in society and felt that Vishnu would sense her drive, being someone who had left this small, suffocating village. This was her one chance to finally get out and she was not going to stop at anything today to have this opportunity pass her by. How many men from this village and from within her caste had made it out of this village? None. None for generations. She got into that pick-up with her parents with complete determination, and they drove on to the grandest house of their small village on the outskirts of the Idukki hills.
~
In prospective groom’s household, Gayatri was managing the chaos as preparations were underway for the meetings she had planned with each family and the bride candidate for her beloved son, Vishnu. The past days were spent sprucing up the house and ensuring that every nook of the house had been cleaned. The Cook had been busy preparing specialty vegetarian dishes, including jackfruit curry and a saffron water-infused rava kesari. Marigold arrangements adorned the courtyard and the main living/dining area where the guests would arrive that morning. The whole atmosphere felt festive.
Gayatri was nervous, despite being the groom’s mother and having the upper-hand. She was amazed by how much Vishnu had grown since he left for Kochi. He was the most eligible bachelor this small village had ever seen and was a catch, being a successful big city businessman. She supervised all arrangements herself, from the most minute detail. She had always wanted the best of the best for her only son and she would not tire until she did.
The whole family had to be presentable today and she had given them strict instructions, including what to wear and how to conduct themselves. She had worked with their maid to ensure all outfits were pressed and ready the night before to avoid any hiccups the next day. She even had a newly tailored suit for Vishnu that had been picked up from the nearby town.
The village mystic cautioned her that he should wear a dark green tie with white stripes to ensure that the day stayed auspicious and to ward off the evil eye from Vishnu and the family. Gayatri, always so clued in to the astrological world, made sure that the tie was exactly that shade of green with those stripes. She even had the maid run over to the village mystic to confirm that the tie met the mystic’s instructions. On the behest of the mystic, Gayatri even had the mystic perform a small ceremony to dispel any bad spirits or ill-intending wishers. Gayatri reasoned it was a tiny price to pay to insure her son’s future.
Finally, when she was satisfied with all the arrangements, Gayatri got herself ready in a pale pink pure crepe silk sari and wore her finest gold jewelry that had been given to her as part of her dowry. Between her eyebrows, she carefully placed a large pink bindi that matched her sari. With that, she looked the part of the “traditional Indian wife.”
In her youth, much like Lakshmi, she was quite the beauty, but her face had tired over the years. The miscarriages, the beatings, the bullying and taunts had taken the life out of her once joyful being. She never received the emotional support that she should have received during those painful times. That morning, she revived a bit of her youthful self with splashes of color to her cheeks and staining her plump lips. She added a light line of kajol to her waterline so that her amber eyes would light up once again. With that, she felt enlivened as she took on the role of a dutiful mother, whose whole life had been dedicated to raise her son and find him a young woman to fill her shoes. She wanted to ensure that her one and only son had all the joys a married life brought.
~
It was finally time for the appointments. The prospective brides began rolling in with their parents in the foyer of the grand house. The village elder and Ramdev were dressed in white silk dhotis. Along with Gayatri, they stood in the entrance to greet each family with a respectful namaskar. The maid ushered each family to sit in order in the guest room that had been temporarily converted into a waiting room for the day.
In the waiting room, Gayatri made sure that the guests understood could visualize what marrying into her family meant. There was a luxurious spread of snacks that included local delicacies, imported European chocolate, and even those shortbread cookies that Lakshmi had only seen at tea time at the cardamom estate. They had green napkins outlined with a gold border, which perfectly complemented the fine bone China plates laid out for the guests to use along with heirloom silver cutlery.
The Cook came by to ask if anyone wanted cardamom tea, water, Limca or a Thums up. They even had the aircon blasting at full to keep the guests cool. And undoubtedly, Gayatri’s ostentatious display did not go unnoticed by the guests. Their eyes grew big as they observed the grandeur of the mini palace in the grandest home of their little village. In their heads, the guests tried to calculate the groom’s family’s vast wealth and couldn’t even imagine what Vishnu’s success in the big city looked like. They were already so impressed by the opulence in front of them that day. Within families, knowing glances were exchanged, heads nodded in mutual understanding, and eyebrows danced in unison with smiles at the expensive décor. The fine silk clothes that the hosts wore hadn’t gone unnoticed either. Every touch boasted wealth.
Lakshmi and her family were equally astonished when they arrived. They knew they were wealthy, but they hadn’t comprehended this until they stepped into the grand house. For a short moment, nervousness returned to Lakshmi as she saw the other candidates. Unlike her, they were wearing finer silk saris and heavier, finer gold jewelry that had been imported from the Middle East. Her family was clearly very poor in comparison to theirs.
Through the village whisperings, she and her family had heard that Vishnu’s family would want at the very least a brand-new car for the family and a motorcycle for Vishnu. Lakshmi’s palms started to sweat and her heart was beating rapidly as these thoughts continued to flood her mind. The thought that her family would not be able to provide a dowry without her father taking out hefty loans from greedy loan sharks worried Lakshmi. She was wearing pretty much all the gold jewelry her mother owned.
Lakshmi had heard her parents chatting over the past few late nights through the thin, poorly insulated walls of their small home. They discussed how they would arrange the dowry in the event that Lakshmi was indeed chosen. Her father had talked to this man in the next village who would offer them a loan that would be paid over 5 years. The lender had said that there would be no interest on the loan for the first six months and then it would be a flat 30% every month from then on. They intended to pay the loan back as quickly as possible to avoid the hefty interest if they could. Her father had decided that he would take on extra trucking jobs and would find additional odd jobs to meet ends where needed. Devi would have to find additional night jobs in the nearby town, but they would make it work with some sacrifice.
Devi saw the sudden change in Lakshmi’s demeanor and took her hand in hers. She blinked holding the blink for a few seconds to provide Lakshmi with reassurance. It was Devi’s silent way of telling her daughter not to worry and to focus on the task of the day, winning Vishnu. Vishnu was Lakshmi’s ticket to her dreams out of this village.
“What if the family asked for more?” Lakshmi thought as she looked at her mother. With Devi’s reassurance, Lakshmi shook the negative thoughts away. “It is a price to pay for moving up in the world. Plus, my mother is right, I can’t worry about that now. I may not even be chosen,” Lakshmi reasoned.
To Devi, it was important to have her daughter married off as young as possible. It was just the icing on the cake that if this match worked out, Lakshmi would marry a rich businessman and have the opportunity to move out of the village. It was a rare opportunity that women like her never got. Plus, Devi knew that her dear friend, Trisha, was right. This was one chance in a blue moon given to Lakshmi and only because Lakshmi was as
beautiful as she was. Devi always knew that her baby girl shone more brightly that any other girl in the village and had brought luck to their family. Devi felt confident like she never had before that Lakshmi was destined to be with Vishnu.
For Lakshmi’s father, a quiet and reserved man, the prospect of her marrying up was equally important. For him, the math was easier. He was glad that his daughter would be married sooner than later as having a daughter around was a burden. It just happened that the marriage prospect was from the wealthiest family of the village. In his mind, any man would have done for Lakshmi. He even thought Krishna would have been a good enough, but Devi would always dismiss the idea of that union. With regards to the dowry, he figured that Lakshmi’s brother and cousin-brother would be able to work in a year or so and would contribute towards paying off the loan as well, if needed. Plus, Arvind figured that if his son’s sister was married into the highest class in their village, then his son would have his pick of girls in the village. Through this alliance, their status would be elevated, and Arvind was pleased that him and his son would receive the respect a man from his class was never afforded or could ever imagine.
~
“Family of Lakshmi,” the maid in her role as “personal assistant” today called on Lakshmi and her parents. She then guided them from the make-shift waiting room through a long passageway along one side of the courtyard and ushered them into a spacious, well-decorated room. They were the fifth of the eight candidates to be interviewed and everyone in the room looked weary from the long hours of small talk and introductions.
Devi thought to herself that it would have been better if they gone first or last. Trisha had said that those would be the most memorable visits to the family and looking at everyone’s tired faces, it appeared so. Perhaps Vishnu’s family had done this on purpose. Her sweet daughter was being just there for them to have some fun among interviewing the real candidates.
Devi then pulled all her energy together to get out of the negative spiral of thoughts, remembering that her daughter was a unique flower among these weeds. “These other candidates may have more money, but they don’t have the class, personality or the charisma to match it. No one can compete with my Lakshmi. A rose among thorns,” she reassured herself as they walked into the grand living and dining room. She held on to that thought, holding her shoulders high, proudly presenting her daughter as did any of the other mothers that day.
“Come, come now. Don’t be shy. Sit here. And you, Lakshmi, over here so we can all see you well. Here, in the light,” Ramdev and Gayatri welcomed Lakshmi and her parents.
They crept forward initially intimidated by the grandeur of the room that was bigger than their two-room house.
“You have a very beautiful daughter. She is very fair and I’m sure you must have nobility somewhere in your ancestry,” Gayatri said as she closely examined Lakshmi.
“Stand up and show us your feet,” Ramdev barked at Lakshmi. He was less pleased by the match, although he allowed his wife to fervently pursue it. “I need to make sure she can walk properly. She seems to be hiding something beneath her sari.”
Lakshmi’s cheeks flushed with embarrassment by the request as she gently lifted her sari to reveal her perfect, slender feet. Despite the raggedness of her slippers, Ramdev nodded that he was satisfied she had no hidden physical defects.
“Now child, tell us a bit about yourself and why you would like to marry our Vishnu,” Gayatri proceeded, diffusing the tension Ramdev’s stunt had created. As she spoke, she turned towards her son that had been quietly seated on an armchair across from Lakshmi and her parents under a dimly lit lamp. All that could be seen from their perspective was a smartly dressed lanky man and the glint of his teeth, one of which was coated in gold.
Lakshmi felt this was some sort of trap. A young lady should be seen and not heard, and should wait for her parents to speak first. At least, that’s what Trisha had instructed her and Devi. Demurely turning to her parents with a delicate smile and a sparkle in her eyes, she looked at them for permission to speak.
Arvind and Devi stepped in with a somewhat rehearsed dialogue that they had worked on with Trisha’s husband and Trisha over the past week. They explained how dear their young daughter was and that she was their lucky charm. Since her birth, neither of them had ever experienced any difficulty as Lakshmi had brought so much luck. They hoped that she would do the same for her future household when the time came. They spoke of how humbled they were to have their daughter chosen to interview for a potential alliance with Vishnu and his family.
They strategically decided to leave out any discussion of their ability to meet any sort of dowry or that their meager living could possibly provide. Their social status was no mystery to anyone there. At most, Arvind and Devi knew they could provide some gold jewelry and the motorbike for Vishnu, but they left this out for if and when, Lakshmi was chosen.
As they spoke, Gayatri noticed the yawns in the room from the very long day. To change up the pace, she asked if Lakshmi could accompany Cook to the kitchen to make some tea as it was nearing 2pm now. It was one of the times during the day that Vishnu and his family sat down for their tea. She made sure
everyone in the room knew how particular her son was about his tea and that only she had ever really been able to make him the perfect cup.
“But no pressure, my dear. Even Cook hasn’t been able to make chai like I do for my handsome boy,” Gayatri said as Lakshmi and Cook walked out to the kitchen.
Devi got nervous at the thought of her daughter having to make tea, when none of the other girls had to. A flood of negative thoughts entered her mind as she sat there quietly, unable to do anything in the situation. “Was this an additional test because they were from a lower class? Was this intended to embarrass them?” Devi thought. Despite this, she maintained her composure with a smile as they sat in now awkward silence with the prospective groom’s family. Arvind seemed unmoved by the situation as to him, it was only customary for women to fetch tea for men and their guests.
As Devi sat there in her thoughts, she remembered what Trisha said about Lakshmi’s god-gifted talents. She was an all-rounder. She wasn’t just blessed in physical beauty, but she could make a bowl of rice taste like a feast fit for a raja. As Devi grounded herself in these truths, she felt relief and remembered how fragrant and delicious Lakshmi’s special cardamom tea had always been. Everyone who came to their house, asked that it be made by Lakshmi, so surely it would match if not beat out Gayatri’s tea.
A short while later, Lakshmi arrived with Cook two steps behind her, holding a fine bone China pot of tea, tea cups and an assortment of shortbread cookies, nuts, and savory snacks on a large tray. Cook put down the freshly-polished silver tray adorned with floral engravings and was about to begin tea service when Gayatri interrupted.
“No, no. Let us have Lakshmi serve the tea. Cook, you can go for now,” Gayatri instructed.
Lakshmi took a deep breath as she could see everyone’s eyes fixated on her. Luckily during her afternoon teas with Kanika madam over the years, she had observed the proper etiquette of serving tea. She asked the most important person in the room first, the village elder, and proceeded down the line in seniority to Ramdev, Gayatri, Vishnu, Vishnu’s family, her father, and then finally, her mother. She asked each one how they liked their tea. After tea was served, she followed on with offering snacks. She was sure not to serve herself at all. And when all done, her cheeks flushed pink with pride for having successfully completed her first tea service.
The chatter amongst the men mainly continued over menial topics such as the local village ordinances and happenings. And as they drank the first sips of tea, the village elder smiled, delighting in its taste. He then cheekily asked his grandson what he thought of the tea. Out of the shadows, the lanky young man emerged grinning.
“Mother,” a deep voice cut through the room. “Don’t be mad now, but this is the most delicious cup of tea I’ve ever had,” Vishnu exclaimed as he caught the demure eyes of Lakshmi and held his gaze. Lakshmi caught his eyes, but quickly averted hers from his. In that quick exchange, she briefly saw her potential suitor that had been so mysterious before that moment. With that glance, Lakshmi felt a warm sensation throughout her body, one that she had never felt before. Her heart beat so much faster that she was scared the people in the room would hear how easily she had been affected.
Gayatri smiled and let out a laugh, followed by, “Oh my, looks like I may have met my match finally.” Gayatri was overjoyed by Lakshmi’s performance and this could be used to further
advocate for her as a match for Vishnu when she later spoke with Ramdev and the family. The village mystic had said their horoscopes were perfectly matched, despite the fact that Lakshmi came from a lower class, and this seemed to reaffirm it.
“That girl may have been born into their class but she is truly a child of the Gods disguised as a pauper,” the village mystic had told Gayatri. “Don’t let that one go, and don’t focus too much on the dowry. That may impede marriage matters. Lakshmi will ensure that your son’s fate roars and he will reach such unbelievable ranks with her by his side. Heed my words, Gayatri, and make sure you convince Ramdev. Your husband is a bit of a knucklehead, but you will need to navigate this alliance sensibly.”
After the tea service was complete, Lakshmi and her parents departed the room and were shown out the front door. As Trisha had advised Devi, Devi was sure not to ask too many questions regarding the next steps and so they left without appearing desperate.
“You don’t want to appear too eager, Devi. They will be put off by that and throw taunts at you because we come from a lower class,” Trisha warned her best friend. And so, Devi, her husband, and Lakshmi left a quarter after 3 that afternoon. They were handed a box of coconut barfi and sent along their way.
~
On the ride back home, Lakshmi felt so proud of herself. No matter what the outcome, she could hold on to the fact that there was nothing more she could have done to make that interview go more smoothly. With Rani’s help, she prepared herself to accept the lowest of expectations going in, but she couldn’t help that gut feeling of full confidence that she had this in the bag.
Comparing herself to competition in terms of beauty and grace, she outshone every girl there. Plus, she was the only girl that had been asked to make tea and her family had stayed at least a half hour more than any of the other girls and their families. And above all, Vishnu had spoken directly to her and praised her special cardamom tea as better than even his own mother’s. What praise!
He gave her the most endearing compliment she had ever received from a man or anyone really. She could feel a warmth fill her body again as she replayed his words in her head. She savored the glimpse she had gotten of her future husband. She felt a sensation between her loins that she had never experienced before as she thought again and again of the moment her and Vishnu’s eyes met. She looked to see if her parents had noticed these passionate feelings she was having. Feeling a bit embarrassed, her cheeks flushed pink.
“But are these thoughts really so bad?” she asked herself. “At the end of the day, he may become my husband. The man I spend the rest of my life with.” She couldn’t help but feel a sense of joy over this possibility and she relished in this happy feeling.
~
The anticipation had been eating at Lakshmi and her family over the past couple of days and the anxiety was only heightened by close friends persistently asking if they had heard anything from the village elder’s household. Trisha spent the last few days comforting Devi as she became restless from the wait. Trisha urged her friend to exercise some patience as they had no control over the outcome. Secretly, Trisha anticipated that the delay was due to a rejection coming Devi’s way and she couldn’t wait to leap at the opportunity to ask Lakshmi’s hand in marriage for her son.
Finally, a golden sealed envelope had arrived by messenger with the family name embossed in elegant calligraphy. That night, the sky was so clear that every star was visible and the moon was full. The face of the moon appeared to be smiling down on Lakshmi’s home in that small village in the Idukki hills. The family, along with Trisha and Rani, gathered closely together in one of their dimly lit rooms. Lakshmi felt her throat dry up and she took a large gulp as her mother pulled out a card adorned by the Lord Ganesha on the cover. Fervent looks were exchanged as Devi flipped open the card and with her poor grasp of reading announced, “A Hearty Congratulations to the Parents of Lakshmi.”
Uncharacteristically, Lakshmi tore the card out of her mother’s hands to read the words herself and save herself from the suspense that had been building over the past days. She stood aback in disbelief as she read out loud that she had been selected. She had won the heart of the most eligible bachelor in their village. She was beyond overjoyed. She was a step closer to moving to Kochi and leaving this boring life. She couldn’t help but let out a series of loud exclamations, “Yes! Yes! Yes!”
Devi was ecstatic that she ran into the kitchen and brought out the gifted box of coconut burfi to sweeten everyone’s mouth in honor of the excellent news. Her daughter was going to get married. Married to not just anyone, but to the richest family and the most successful son the village had ever seen. She always knew her daughter was destined for great things and this news proved it.
Trisha was shell-shocked. She masked the pangs of jealousy with a broad, fake smile of support. “What will I do with Krishna, now?” she silently questioned herself as she stood there. She then took the card that had been placed aside to read the news herself and to confirm this was not just her imagination.
“A Hearty Congratulations to the Parents of Lakshmi. We cordially invite you to our home this Saturday evening to formalize the engagement of our dear son, Vishnu, and our future daughter-in-law, Lakshmi.” Lakshmi and her family, Trisha and Rani spent the rest of the evening celebrating and chatting about next steps, which Trisha painfully set aside her utter disappointment through the festivities.
Lakshmi’s grandmother and Devi held each other in a strong embrace, something they otherwise never did. Both of them were overjoyed by the prospect of the marriage, although both for different reasons. Lakshmi’s grandmother couldn’t contain the pure relief that her granddaughter would be the first of a long line of women from this village to escape. The invitation from the village elder’s household was more than just a prospect for marriage. It represented freedom. Freedom from the shackles of ordinariness of the lives of women born into a lowly rank in a small village on the outskirts of Idukki had to bear from.
For Devi, she was thrilled that her daughter was to be married so young and into such a prestigious household. Devi’s status amongst the women would be automatically elevated. Devi could unabashedly gloat to all the other village women of how they were blessed with Lakshmi, the namesake of the Goddess herself, after years of them taunting her over her miscarriages and ill fortunes. For Devi, however, the cherry on top was that when the time came for her son to marry, he would have his pick as all the women who had previously stuck up their noses at her, would be parading their daughters in front of her.
Lakshmi and Rani huddled together on Lakshmi’s bed that was in the far corner of the room she shared with her mother and grandmother, fervently chatting and giggling away at Lakshmi’s good news. When they were younger, they had always known
that they were not going to stay in this small village and that one day, they were both going to leave. The girls had big dreams for themselves. Rani knew that Lakshmi would always be the first to leave. She was so much prettier and didn’t have the responsibility of younger siblings like she did. Rani was truly overjoyed for her friend, but couldn’t help but compare how her life was not nearly half as free and fortunate as Lakshmi’s.
“If it wasn’t for my mother dying and my god-awful father who comes home drunk from who knows where, I too could have been chosen for Vishnu. I’m just as fair as Lakshmi, and if I grew my hair long, just as stunning if not more. People would be enthralled by my shapely body. These breasts. Mine are so voluptuous and full in comparison to those small mangos she has poking out of her kameez,” Rani thought to herself while giggling sillily away with her best friend atop of Lakshmi’s bed.
As they chatted, the evening turned into the early morning hours and finally, Trisha and Rani left to their respective homes. Lakshmi and her family turned in for the night, lying in their beds, but no one able to sleep given the rush of excitement that the news in that little gold envelope gave. Today, they felt truly blessed as they wrestled thoughts of joy and anticipation of what was to come next with the need to sleep.
~
By the morning, the news had spread like wild fire and the village ladies circled Lakshmi and her family like vultures. They barged in, without invitation, into Devi’s and Arvind’s home and began what seemed to be an inquest. When Lakshmi went out to fetch water, she was crowded by inquiring tongues. She narrowly escaped to sit in the Gangalee fields, a small reprieve she especially enjoyed today as she sat there reveling in the news she received the night before. As she looked around, she
noticed that the flowers seemed to burst with an extra splash of happiness as their gentle purple hue lit up in the sun’s rays. Lakshmi took a few deep breaths as she inhaled the sweet, delicate fragrance and admired the moments of peacefulness surrounding her. She relished in her prospects without the barrage of questions from the village ladies as she lay face up, looking up at the clouds and smiling with birds flying overhead and the sun blessing her with its warmth. She felt truly happy in that moment of mindfulness.
~
Somehow, Kanika madam had heard the news of Lakshmi’s upcoming engagement and called an impromptu mid-week afternoon tea while they were at the Idukki Eco Farm for their regular shift. She was so excited and wanted to hear every detail. Kanika madam was fascinated by the lives of the girls that lived in their tiny homes in the small village on the outskirts of Idukki. Today, she had even asked Mamta to bring an eggless Black Forest cake for the girls to enjoy and with her mother, had picked one of her finest rose-colored saris with a delicate silver trim and a small gold aum pendant and chain to gift Lakshmi ahead of her engagement. Lakshmi tried to return the gift, but Kanika and her mother insisted. They said that she could not possible refuse this or anything they gave for her upcoming nuptials. The other village girls couldn’t help but feel envious at Lakshmi’s good fortune.
“Lakshmi, tell me everything. What did you wear? What does he look like? Is he tall, dark and handsome?” Kanika questioned in rapid fire. As she learned he was a businessman from Kochi, Kanika madam stopped to think. She hadn’t heard of him or even the company he was an executive for, but of course, she reasoned that Kochi was such a big city and what would she
know anyway. She was just a pretty thing and didn’t really bother herself with the business world despite being dragged around by her parents from one soiree to another. Business was for Kanika madam’s father and her brothers. She was a socialite. As Kanika madam inquired, Lakshmi thought to herself that perhaps one day she too would bump into Kanika madam at one of these so-called “swarays.”
The girls slowly moved on to one of their favorite topics, Mills & Boon type stories. The girls naughtily discussed how Lakshmi would be the first of them to experience the pleasures described in these romance novels. In fact, they felt that Lakshmi’s story was a real-life fairy tale. All the girls giggled together and blushed as Kanika madam pulled out one of these romance books and read a steamy passage she had tabbed. The girls were too innocent to know the full meaning behind these words or what emotions were being evoked, but the passion described in the novels seemed so magical that they couldn’t wait for the day that they experienced such pleasures.
~
That date for Lakshmi’s engagement was determined by the village mystic. It was the upcoming Saturday. Very soon. Lakshmi’s family had been instructed not to worry about the typical formalities that the bride’s family traditionally was responsible such as the rings, gifts of fine clothing, food and anything of that nature. Trisha and Rani assembled at Devi’s house ahead of the event of the season, advising mother and daughter ahead of the most talked about event in their village.
As advised, Lakshmi continued the prescription of turmeric face and body masks as well as coconut oil in her hair. The night before, Rani had come in again and painted Lakshmi’s delicate nails that had been disallowed from being exposed to
any household chores since the announcement. Lakshmi had also been instructed to take things easy while working at the cardamom farm.
The day of, Lakshmi washed and Rani generously massaged delicate amounts of coconut oil over her body, hands and face. Her mother applied the Gangalee oil she had prepared on the scent spots as she had done previously and added a splash in Lakshmi’s thick locks.
Lakshmi’s grandmother watched from afar as Lakshmi got ready. She had kept her wedding sari that she hadn’t even let her own daughter wear for Lakshmi with the hopes that she would wear it on this important day. But now she silently worried it wouldn’t meet the standards of Vishnu and his family. Even though she was initially pleased with the news, she secretly worried how Lakshmi would manage in a household so different from theirs. Unlike others, she was also not convinced that Vishnu had really changed from his childhood days, but she kept these thoughts to herself. To reassure herself, she reminded herself that, “At least Lakshmi can escape this small prison we women have been living in from one generation to the next. Our Lakshmi, dear sweet, innocent flower. From the outside she looks so dainty, but she is a strong warrior like me. Not like her mother at all. She will be more than fine. Prayers for my sweet child. Protect her, Goddess Lakshmi.”
“Wow, wow, look at this sari. Kanika madam has such taste. How fortunate to be gifted this at this opportune time. You are going to look even more stunning than the last time they saw you,” Rani exclaimed, hiding her envy with a big false smile and forced excitement. In that moment, Lakshmi’s grandmother hid away her wedding sari that was dull and cheap looking in comparison to the one gifted by Kanika madam.
“Hahn, Rani. This small village girl has transformed into a true queen. No other has matched her beauty in decades since,” Trisha replied. Rani nodded gleefully at that comment, feeling the sting of what she saw as a falsehood as she stood there, just as pretty. Just as fair. It should have been her if not for her short hair.
Secretly, Trisha hoped that this was just a cruel joke being played out by the wealthiest family of the village and that Lakshmi was still just entertainment. She hoped that they would humiliate Lakshmi today and Devi and Lakshmi would come home crying in disgrace. Trisha would then swoop in and save her best friend’s daughter and the family’s dignity by offering up her son, Krishna, as a suitable alternative. “All in good time. Patience. Patience, Trisha,” Trisha thought holding out hope.
Devi and Trisha busily worked to tie Lakshmi’s sari, this time revealing just a bit more of her midriff to keep Vishnu interested. They adorned Lakshmi with the beautiful pendant and chain that Kanika madam had gifted her and some teardrop earrings that Krishna picked up from the store when he was tasked to get the gajra again, but this time for the engagement.
Lakshmi’s father sat with Lakshmi’s brother and cousin brother playing a game of carrom. He was overjoyed at the prospect of his daughter getting married. Finally, she would settle down in her own family’s house and no longer be a burden on them. He was anxious, however, of all the upcoming and sudden costs that they would have to take on. The dowry that Vishnu’s family would demand would be far greater than a prospect from their class would ever demand. If they could not fulfill the dowry, he was concerned that Vishnu’s family would turn down his daughter and this would bring irreversible shame unto his family.
“Look at how this foolish peacock strutted his feathers when the village elder called for his daughter’s hand in marriage,” the villagers would say.
“He was so proud and had his nose up held so high in the air, but he should have thought of his status as a laborer. No match for the wealthy,” they would go on.
“Who would have these spoiled goods now? They should have not accepted such an invitation, but they were so vain. A pretty girl she is, but that cannot hide her poverty and dismal upbringing,” they would shamelessly say directly to his face if the engagement went sour.
Devi, on the other hand, was no longer worried. She had quietly consulted the village mystic upon receipt of the invitation and the mystic had told Devi not to worry either and to have faith. She assured Devi that this girl was going to shine so bright that nothing could possibly hold her back. She advised, however, that Devi should tie a black string around her daughter’s left foot’s little toe before going to the engagement in addition to putting a discrete black mark behind Lakshmi’s ear.
“You don’t want people seeing the mark to ward off their evil eye. This little gesture will protect your daughter and everything will go successfully. I only see success. Absolute success. And with their union, even more success in her future. Don’t worry, Devi. Have I ever been wrong?” the village mystic told Devi with a confident smile. Devi blindly believed in the village mystic as many in the village did. The village mystic had always been right and if it wasn’t for her advising Devi on the special potions to drink and prayers to conduct, Devi would never have conceived Lakshmi in the first place.
In addition to this, the village mystic also sold Devi a special potion that Devi and not Lakshmi had to drink at the first sight
of the moon every night from the night before the engagement and all up to the day of Lakshmi’s marriage. She warned that if Devi did not do this religiously, then there could be delays or bad consequences that could affect Lakshmi’s marriage prospects. In Devi’s mind, this was a small price to pay to secure her daughter’s future.
“There is no way my daughter is not going to marry into that family and get out of this pathetic village. She was born a beauty and has been our family’s lucky charm. I will not disappoint our deities. No money, no ritual, no nothing is going to stop us from realizing this dream for our sweet daughter,” she thought to herself with full gumption.
~
The blush pink sari perfectly complemented Lakshmi’s complexion and she looked so elegant. She looked like an upper-class woman gracing the needy as she walked past curious villages who crowded her and her parents as they climbed into the pick-up. Lakshmi was more nervous this time around as she felt more was at stake. Although she knew she looked stunning, she had to continue to charm and please them. One slip and the opportunity could be taken away from her. She reminded herself that she was naturally charming, but she separately worried about the dowry demands that Vishnu’s family would have. She worried about the burden her marriage would bring to her parents.
This guilt subsided quickly as her thoughts wondered to the fantastic opportunities she would have once she moved to Kochi. She could easily use her femininity and natural wit to charm Vishnu into helping out her parents with the dowry payments. In the small glimpse the two had exchanged that day, Lakshmi felt Vishnu’s kind and generous soul through his deep hazel brown
eyes. She felt at ease now, reminiscing over their short, dreamy exchange and as usual, she was lost in her thoughts and hadn’t realized that they had arrived.
“Lakshmi, Lakshmi! Child, wake up! Let’s go, we’re here now,” Devi sternly said in a hushed tone as she tapped Lakshmi’s shoulder. Devi did so as quietly as she could but enough to jolt Lakshmi back to reality. Devi was always amazed at how easily Lakshmi went into her dream world, especially on a day like today and with Vishnu and his family standing right at the front of the house to welcome them into their grand house.
Gayatri had her trusty puja plate out and ready. “My future daughter-in-law, you look absolutely stunning in that beautiful pink sari. My son is a very lucky man. May you both be happy and may evil never touch either of you,” Gayatri said as she proceeded to mumble prayers and then she ushered the family into their house. Lakshmi walked in, remembering to enter with her right foot which was considered auspicious. Gayatri noticed and was pleased by Lakshmi’s attention and devotion to their religious customs. Gayatri then directed them to the Tulsi plant that sat in the center of the courtyard, a fixture for several generations now. She asked Lakshmi to pour water from a silver pot to feed the sacred plant as Gayatri and the household sang a Hindu prayer. Once the prayers and rituals were completed, the family settled into the living / dining room.
~
This time Lakshmi was seated right next to Vishnu on the sofa amongst large pleated maroon cushions, encased in decorative silk covers, embroidered with colorful bird scenes. The sofa’s fabric was so gentle on Lakshmi’s skin. She hadn’t noticed during her first visit due to her nerves, but today she sank into them. They were a stark comparison to the hard, but versatile
woven cots they sat on, ate on, dried chilies on, and at night, slept on, in their home.
“Vah, what a couple they make. Picture perfect. Don’t you all agree? I’ve never seen a better-looking union,” Gayatri exclaimed as she smiled towards Ramdev, Ramdev’s father, and the extended family. Devi and her husband nodded their heads in agreement, with Arvind smiling in unison, but dreading the questions around dowry to come.
Ramdev stepped into the conversation in that moment, “Now that we have a bride for Vishnu, we need to make arrangements for the wedding. Gayatri consulted the village mystic and she has checked their horoscopes. This match is perfect, but the wedding must take place soon. She indicated that next month will be most auspicious. The wedding should take place on the Thursday in early October between the hours of six and nine in the morning.” Ramdev was a no non-sense type of guy and managed the discussions around marriage as if it were one of his many business dealings.
Devi and her husband listened intently, pleased with the suggestion but also anxious with the tight timeline. It would be wonderful to have their dear sweet daughter settled into her new family before Diwali. Lakshmi would then celebrate the occasion in style in her new household. However, the stressful matter of the dowry hadn’t yet been broached.
Lakshmi’s parents had discussed on end how they would borrow the money and pay the dowry off by working extra shifts and jobs, but they hadn’t realized the pressure of the situation until these marriage discussions with the wealthiest family in the village were now taking place. They didn’t dare to contest the timing or make any suggestions that could possibly upset the groom’s family. They couldn’t face the backlash if Vishnu’s family became upset and broke off the engagement in that
moment. That would leave their daughter and their family disgraced. The thought of the dowry demands made Lakshmi’s father sweat.
