“We heard there was some sort of altercation yesterday at the hotel where you and Vishnu worked. With some chef there?” she inquired further, the Inspector woman’s eyes squinting as she looked deep into Lakshmi’s eyes.
“Yes, ma’am. I dance as a Kochi Queen at the hotel and in my line of work, I have many male admirers. Chef Vijaya worked at the hotel but crossed the line. He gave me this locket and letter. The girls in my group told my husband about it and it made him angry. Vishnu beat up the chef and the chef was asked to leave,” Lakshmi said holding back tears that she manufactured in that moment. Ruby and Lakshmi had discussed coming clean up front with the police as they’d most certainly find out the details from Mrs. Iyer, if not from Kalika and Sita if a further inquiry was made.
“Yes, yes. That is what we heard too. We were able to confirm Chef Vijaya’s whereabouts last night and this morning. He was admitted to a local ER as he had a concussion and was discharged this morning, so we see no connection there,” the Inspector woman replied. In her mind, the poor sweet girl that sat before her had suffered a great tragedy so early in her years. The Inspector woman found Lakshmi to be so stunningly beautiful that it would surprise no one she would have admirers chasing after her. After all, her husband was a drunkard who lived the poors’ lifestyle, making his heart attack shocking but not out of the ordinary. The Inspector woman considered whether it was worth her station’s time and resources to waste funding on such an open and shut case. At the end of the day, the Inspector knew that whatever the case may be, the poor young woman would be vilified as a young widow by her own people even if she or Chef Vijaya had a hand in Vishnu’s death.
Lakshmi and Ruby sat there quietly for the next question from the Inspector woman as she flipped through a folder. At that moment, a clumsy Gopal came through the door with a piping hot cup of Madras coffee. “Sugar?” the Inspector woman said as she turned from the folder to Gopal and pointed to the cup.
“Yes, ma’am. Two teaspoons, stirred in just like you like it,” Gopal replied diligently.
“Excellent. After my coffee, I’m headed home. These girls can go to Tinku. And when the autopsy report comes, file it with the report please and close the case.” the Inspector woman said and then instructed the girls, “We’re done here. You can go now. Again, sorry for your loss.”
The women thanked her profusely for her time and Gopal led them to the case manager who sat at a desk at the end of the corridor. “Tinku here will help you with next steps,” Gopal said as he slapped the folder down on the desk of this lanky bespeckled man in a worn-out tan suit and awfully long tie. Gopal said good-bye, offered the women his condolences, and abruptly left.
~
Tinku was sat there in piles and piles of army green case file folders, many of which were gathering dust, stacked high around him. Ruby spoke, “Tinku sir.” But she was interrupted as Tinku raised his knobby finger telling her to pause there, without even looking at her.
After a good ten minutes of Lakshmi and Ruby just standing there as Tinku scrolled through a complex system and slowly inputted numbers into a beaten-up desktop computer, Tinku turned. “Case No. 10001928324. Myocardial Infarction.” The girls looked confused at Tinku as he explained, “Death of one, Vishnu. Yours or your husband, brother?” Lakshmi pointed to herself and muttered quietly, “My husband.”
Tinku nodded his head and then said, “Come back in two working days and we will release him to you. Please make the
funeral arrangements by then. Case has been closed by Inspector Radhika Nair.” The girls nodded feeling a sense of great calm and asked Tinku what they needed to do next. He stamped some paper, wrote in a date and time, and Tinku handed them the stamped paper. “Make sure you make the arrangements by then, otherwise the government will cremate him on your behalf.” Abruptly, Tinku then put a “Return shortly” sign on his desk and without any pleasantries left the girls standing by his desk as he headed out for his lunch hour. The station was seemingly quiet now with most of the officers out on their midday break. Lakshmi and Ruby looked at each other and let out a sigh of relief. They hurried out of the station into the marketplace.
“Let’s get far from the station and go somewhere to eat,” Lakshmi urged Ruby. “I’m so hungry, but I don’t want to think of all of this just yet. I’m not ready to plan a funeral. What will I tell everyone back home?” All of a sudden, Lakshmi started hyperventilating at the thought of being a young widow and the thought of being shunned by all of the people she grew up around in her small village on the outskirts of the Idukki hills. She remembered how terribly they treated her cousin brother when both his parents had died, calling him the devil and unlucky for having caused his parents’ deaths although he had no hand in it. She lamented over how the widowed women in her village were ousted and forced to live away at a special ashram hidden from society. The torture that would come from the taunts and spite. She worried in that moment how her parents, grandmother and siblings would be harassed. She broke out into a flood of uncontrollable tears outside the station.
“I can never go back, Ruby. I can’t go back. But I must take him home to his parents so they can perform his last rites. But I can’t live there anymore. I’m a pariah,” Lakshmi wept as she took
short breaths and held onto Ruby’s forearm so tightly. “Don’t ever leave me, Ruby. Don’t leave my side. Ever.”
~
Ruby texted Dravid to let him know that they’d make their own way home and would see him in the evening. Lakshmi begged Ruby to take her on the ferry to Fort Kochi as she had still never been on a boat before despite them having now been there a year now. She desperately needed to run away from her surroundings.
“I’m just not ready to tell the folks at home, Ruby,” she pleaded. “Let’s ring the family when we are home tonight, Ruby. You’ll be my side all the way, my sister. They will brandish me as unlucky and turn their backs on me and my family. What have I done to deserve this?”
Ruby consoled her friend as they boarded the ferry and found a spot on the deck on the port side.  It was a busy time of the day and they were smushed next to the other passengers, some locals commuting, some leisure seekers and others were tourists visiting from other states or from foreign countries. When the ferry left the dock, Lakshmi held onto the rail tightly and took in the fresh salty breeze from the Arabian Sea. Even though the waters were slightly choppy, she felt a closeness to the water. A serenity that she never felt on land. The two friends stood on the ferry in silence and as they approached the jetty of Fort Kochi, Lakshmi grew excited and like a small child in a candy shop, she started jumping up and down and tugging at Ruby.
“Look at that. Look at the buildings! It is so beautiful. So different from anything I’ve ever seen,” Lakshmi exclaimed.
Ruby couldn’t help but smile despite their current situation. She was so happy to see such pure joy in Lakshmi’s eyes after
so many months. Ruby had come here several times over the years, on dates with Dravid or for odd jobs. She always made it a point to stop in at St. Francis Church. Even though she was a loose Hindu and certainly not an Anglican, there was something magnificent and elegant about the architecture of the church. She couldn’t wait to show the church and its grounds to Lakshmi, but first they would get something to eat. She was also starting to feel hungry after having sat anxious most of the day at the police station.
“Let’s find something to eat, then I’ll show you around,” Ruby said. “Then we can enjoy the sunset before heading back.”
It was all the two women wanted in that moment. A sense of escape from the impending doom they’d have to face that evening. They grabbed some snacks at a street food stall and headed to the beach to eat.
“One day, Ruby, when all this is behind us, you and I will dress up and go and eat at a nice, fancy restaurant. I’ve only ever eaten at canteens or road-side cafes, but those seaside restaurants look like something else,” Lakshmi remarked. That spark for a better life came sweeping back and lit up her face. They both smiled at each other and after having had something to satisfy their hunger, they walked over to St. Francis Church.
“Anytime I come to Fort Kochi, my dear sister, I always visit here. You know, the Portuguese built this and Vasco Da Gama, a famous Portuguese explorer, was buried here for some time,” Ruby explained to her as she delved into the bits and pieces of history she knew about the place before the two went into the grand church with an extravagant wooden ceiling and ornate stained-glass windows. They found an empty bench and sat down, where the two prayed in silence as they were surrounded by the church’s peacefulness.
“Dear Goddess Lakshmi, I know that I sit in this church today. A church of another god, but you are all one in my mind. Please give me strength to manage the days ahead and once they are behind me, help me to go to lands like Portugal and places I would never otherwise see. Oh, give me strength, my Goddess,” Lakhsmi sat there with her eyes closed, her head bowed towards the altar and with utmost concentration. “Hear my prayers and guide me as you always have.”
After sometime, the two women walked around and admired the architecture of the church before going outside and looking at the placards around the church grounds. They then headed down to the beach again where they found a spot with a throng of others to watch the sun as it slowly set. The sky changed from a cool clear blue sky, slowly burning deep red-orange. The colors softened into a purplish pink hue and the crescent moon was now visible to the naked eye. The two women sat in silence enjoying tranquility after the calamity of the past 24 hours. After some time of meditative rest, the women got up and walked back to the jetty to take the ferry home.
~
Once back at the apartment, Lakshmi sat on the sofa while Ruby put some tea on and called Dravid to come over as soon as he could. Lakshmi pulled up her mother’s contact details on her phone. They had only spoken intermittently since she had moved to Kochi. Lakshmi had done what she thought was right to protect her family from finding out too many details about her new life, slowly distanced herself from them. She hadn’t spoken to her mother now in a couple of weeks but they had texted every day. “Good morning, Mama. Yes, I’m doing okay. No, I’m not pregnant yet, but I tried the remedy the mystic suggested. Good night, Mama.” Her family had no idea what life she was living
in Kochi. They probably assumed she was living the high life of a socialite with less time for her family back home, in the small village on the outskirts of the Idukki hills.
It was getting late on a Tuesday evening. Lakshmi wasn’t even sure if her father was home that day or if he was off on one of his trucking jobs. She hesitated and couldn’t get herself to make the call. Ruby brought the tea over and some Parle-G cookies. They heard a knock on the door. It was Dravid. The three of them sat together in silence, paralyzed. Lakshmi didn’t know how to break the news to her parents. Dravid didn’t know how to tell Vishnu’s parents either. They had been friends for so long now. Ruby sat there helpless too, not knowing how to help either of them.
After an hour of inaction, Ruby took the phone from Lakshmi and pressed the call button on Lakshmi’s phone. It started to ring and it continued to ring. No one picked up and so Ruby hung up. She turned to Dravid and took his phone from him and was about to try Vishnu’s parents from Dravid’s phone. Lakshmi stopped her.
“I need to tell my parents first. I need them to know so they can confront what is to come their way,” Lakshmi said finally finding words. Lakshmi texted her mother to call her as soon as she could as it was urgent. She wanted to text her friend, Rani, but the two had spoken less and less over time as Lakshmi hid the reality of her new life. The trio returned sitting there on the couch silently, waiting. Their teas now cold and the biscuits untouched.
A half hour later or so the silence was broken. Lakshmi’s phone was ringing. They looked at each other. Lakshmi didn’t know what to do, so Ruby picked up. “Hello, hello,” they heard on the other side of the receiver.
Ruby cleared her throat and replied, “Um, ah, hello Aunty. This is Ruby. Lakshmi’s friend.” She could hear Lakshmi’s mother frantically asking if Lakshmi was okay. “Yes, yes. Lakshmi is okay. Um, Aunty. I’m sorry to tell you this, but Vishnu. Ah um. He passed away from a heart attack yesterday.” Ruby heard nothing but silence on the other end. Lakshmi was looking inquisitively at her, but Ruby just stared back at her blankly. Then, Ruby heard Lakshmi’s mother’s concern on the other end of the line, “How is Lakshmi? Can I speak to my daughter?” Ruby handed the phone to Lakshmi who began to wail. Ruby could feel Lakshmi’s mother and her grandmother silent and in shock as they heard their little blossom break down in tears.
Ruby took the phone from Lakshmi and said, “Aunty. We will need to tell your in-laws. I’ll have Dravid give them a call now before it gets too late.” Devi could be heard on the receiver telling Ruby to take care of her daughter and that she should come back home soon. Ruby told her that they would be making funeral arrangements, but first would speak to the in-laws and would be in touch with them with details on next steps.
Never having done anything like this before, the trio sat there again in silence. Dravid now had to make the dreaded call to Vishnu’s parents. He rang their number again having not heard back from them. Cook picked up. “Hello, hello, may I know who is speaking?” Dravid let her know that it was him and that he urgently needed to speak with Aunty or Uncle. Cook could sense the urgency in his voice and called out to Gayatri who happened to be nearby. Dravid was half hoping that he could speak to Uncle instead. It was such a terrible thing to break to a mother that her only son that she had fought so hard for, for so long, had passed away at such a tender age, and from a heart attack of all things. He tried to beckon to Cook to hand the phone over to Uncle instead but it was too late as she had handed the
phone over to Gayatri Aunty. Dravid broke the news as gently as he could to Gayatri Aunty and he heard absolute silence on the other end of the line.
Gayatri had fainted with a very confused Cook trying to attend to her. Cook then picked up the phone to try and understand what had transpired. “Hello, hello, Dravid sahib. What has happened? Gayatri di has fainted.” Dravid told her the bad news and Cook lost all color in her face. Dravid explained the circumstances of Vishnu’s death and that they were now trying to console his young widow and make arrangements for the funeral after the police inquiry closed that day. He promised that he would call in the morning to sort out next steps.
Dravid told the girls that he would call Aunty and Uncle tomorrow morning to see if they’d like the body to be sent back to the small village on the outskirts of Idukki Hills or if they would like they to take care of arrangements in Kochi. He then wished them goodnight, with Ruby telling him that she’d stay with Lakshmi, which she knew he would not protest. Once he left, the two girls sat in silence, with Lakshmi cuddled up in Ruby’s arms. Eventually, they both dozed off and were woken the next day by the rays of the sun shining through into the living room.
~
When they woke, there were several missed calls on Lakshmi’s phone, from her parents, relatives, Rani and Maa ji and Papa ji. Lakshmi was too paralyzed to take any of the calls, so Ruby handled them all morning long. Everyone was in initial shock and Vishnu’s family wanted his body back home immediately so Vishnu could be sent off properly to the afterlife.
“Yes, uh-huh. Of course, Aunty. Ah-huh. Yes, Uncle. Thank you for speaking with Mr. Iyer. We will make the arrangements with Dravid and Mr. Iyer. I will come with Lakshmi. Yes, understood,” Ruby could be heard saying.
As the reality of being a widow sunk in, Lakshmi retreated into the bedroom, which was dark and dismal, still plagued by the terrors that Vishnu inflicted on her. She felt detached from reality. “What have I done. I’ve made a worse life for myself and my family,” she thought to herself. “They will blame me and the villagers will ex communicate me to that ashram. Why did I do this to myself>” She cried in the bed. Exhausted, she slowly drifted away on the bed.
Ruby came in to check in on her and told her she had made something light for her to eat and put some tea on the stove. Ruby told Lakshmi that she’d come as soon as they made all the arrangements with the coroner’s office, and that Mr. Iyer had arranged transport to the village for all of them for tomorrow. Lakshmi was too fragile to acknowledge Ruby, but listened to the next steps.
“When times are tough, one day at a time,” she remembered the voice of her grandmother giving this advice when she was impatient. Despite this advice, she was not looking forward to seeing her family or Vishnu’s or anyone else. She was, however, looking forward to being in the comforting embrace of her mother’s and grandmother’s arms.
Ruby returned later that evening with Dravid and Mr. and Mrs. Iyer. Lakshmi hadn’t moved from the bed or eaten what Ruby had left out. Ruby and Mrs. Iyer helped Lakshmi out of bed and to wash her face. They tried to feed her and get some liquids into her system. She ate small morsels mechanically with a blank stare looking in the distance.
“Poor girl,” Mrs. Iyer said as she pulled back a strand of hair that had fallen in front of Lakshmi’s face. “All the light in our Kochi Queen’s face has left. Our sweet little beauty.” Mrs. Iyer was concerned for Lakshmi as she had grown very fond of the girl. She had never been fond of Vishnu and with everything that Vishnu did, even his death had upended the successful event business Mrs. Iyer had grown with the Kochi Queens. In particular, with Lakshmi as their star. Their lucky charm was distraught. Then and there, Mrs. Iyer got busy by first unpacking a week’s worth of white salwar kameez and saris that she had brought with her. “You will need to wear these during the mourning period, my child,” Mrs. Iyer said. For the first time that evening, Lakshmi turned and looked into Mrs. Iyer’s eyes with such utter devastation. Mrs. Iyer shook her head feeling Lakshmi’s pain. “She is like a daughter to me and she should not have to go through such a tragedy like this so young. What a shame,” Mrs. Iyer thought to herself as she shook her head and reached over to Lakshmi to hold her tightly.
After getting Lakshmi back into bed, Ruby, Dravid and the Iyers discussed tomorrow’s itinerary. Ruby and Dravid had made the necessary arrangements that day, and the body was to be released tomorrow morning. After the procession back to the village, the cremation and funeral rites would be performed early the day following that by Vishnu’s family. With everything in place, they all decided to meet up in the early hours of the morning and head to that village on the outskirts of the Idukki hills. Everyone was exhausted from the long couple of days following Vishnu’s death. Ruby stayed with Lakshmi, while everyone left. She dozed off next to Lakshmi, not having eaten anything herself that evening.
~
It was very early the next morning. No one had really gotten any sleep and were still looking very ragged. Mr. and Mrs. Iyer were going to meet them in an hour, so Ruby busied herself around Lakshmi’s apartment making food for the morning and for the journey up ahead. She got Lakshmi ready, having her bathe and change into a white sari. Even in this, Lakshmi looked effervescent although the usual light that accompanied her had dulled. Lakshmi didn’t touch her breakfast. Her nerves were so high and fear of confronting her family, her in-laws and the villagers was eating her away inside. The thought of food nauseated her.
Ruby turned to Lakshmi seeing her lost in these thoughts and said, “Lakshmi, my dear sister, I will be with you every step of the way. You must be strong, and it will be over before you know it.” With that encouragement, Lakshmi felt a bit of peace.
Shortly thereafter, Dravid joined them and sat in silence until his phone buzzed. Mr. and Mrs. Iyer were downstairs ready to take them. Dravid picked up the suitcase for Ruby and himself, as well as the one for Lakshmi, now containing only mourning clothes of white, and headed downstairs. Ruby picked up the food she had prepared for the journey and signaled to Lakshmi to rise from the sofa and head down to the car.
The sun had not risen yet, and they could still see the full moon shining down at them and the stars twinkling. It was the clearest sky they had seen in a long while. Lakshmi sat at the back of the car with Ruby and Mrs. Iyer, as Dravid climbed into the passenger seat next to Mr. Iyer. It was highly unusual for Mr. Iyer to be doing the driving, but these were unusual circumstances. Everyone sat silently as Mr. Iyer put on a radio
channel playing morning religious hymns on low volume in the background as they begun their journey.
Lakshmi was sat in the middle sandwiched between Ruby and Mrs. Iyer. She looked on blankly through the front window of the car as they left the vicinity of Kochi. She could hear Mr. Iyer tell Dravid that they should make good time given that they were leaving before any traffic hit the morning rush hour and that halfway, they should stop at a rest stop and then Dravid could drive the rest of the way. And as Lakshmi sat there staring blankly through the front window, the scene slowly changed from the city roads to the interstate highway, to the regional highway until they stopped at a roadside eatery. Everyone got out to stretch their legs, but Lakshmi sat there like a lifeless body. The blood from her face had receded and she was pale as a sheet as they inched closer and closer to the small village on the outskirts of Idukki hills.
Mr. and Mrs. Iyer, Ruby, and Dravid sat at the tables, leaving Lakshmi in the car and giving her the space that she needed. Ruby and Mrs. Iyer went to use the ladies ‘porta-potty’ and when they came back, Mr. Iyer and then later Dravid went to relieve themselves in the field. They ordered chai all around and Ruby went to check to see if Lakshmi needed anything, but was unable to get much out of her. She made sure Lakshmi sipped some water, before she returned to the group to have her tea. They all sat pretty quietly making the odd comment here and there about the clean air of the surrounding farm land. Ruby had never been to this part of the state and wished she was going to Lakshmi’s hometown under different circumstances.
Dravid received a call that Vishnu’s body would be reaching in an hour and he informed the rest of the party. After sitting there for a few more moments, they got the courage to head back into the car and carry onwards to meet Vishnu’s family, meeting
them under such grim circumstances. With that realization, the rest of the drive was pin drop silent.
~
Upon arrival at Lakshmi’s in-laws’ home, Ruby held her friend’s hand as they stepped out of the car. Lakshmi had covered her head and looked down as she approached her in-laws’ grand house. It didn’t look so grand anymore to her. Cook was the only person waiting outside to receive them. When Lakshmi approached, she gave her a warm, consoling hug.
In these past couple of days, Lakshmi had grown frail. Ruby knew it wasn’t from Vishnu’s passing, but from the fear of being a young widow and from the impending reproachment by the villagers. They walked through the front area, where in the far corner Dumki and Chumki were grazing, seemingly unaware of the events around them. They entered this grand house with the large courtyard, with the Tulsi plant in the center. Ruby had never seen anything so lavish in a small village like this before, and was quite shocked by the comfortable circumstances of Vishnu’s upbringing. “Why would he ever leave this life?” she questioned, and then answered her own question remembering who he was.
Cook directed Mr. Iyer and Dravid to the large living room where the village elder, Ramdev, male relatives, and some of the men of the village, including Lakshmi’s father, had gathered. Lakshmi was directed to a smaller room that had been readied as the mourning area for the women. Ruby directed Lakshmi to a spot that Ruby instinctively knew was for the widow. Lakshmi was sat by a distraught older woman wailing loudly. It was Gayatri.
“My son. My only son,” she was crying and beating her chest as her sister-in-law tried to console her. And soon after their
arrival, Vishnu’s body was delivered. His body was first taken to the men’s mourning room, where the head priest performed rituals and read prayers necessary to ready Vishnu’s soul for the afterlife. The women could hear the cries of the men and soon the same somber atmosphere was heard with wails and screams of the women. Devi and her grandmother were seated far in the corner, shunned by all the village women. Ruby saw that Devi kept looking at her daughter, hoping to see her face even for a minute. Devi’s heart sank for all the pain that Lakshmi would have to endure and the banishment from her family and her past life in the village.
Soon, in the late morning hours, a procession took Vishnu to a pyre that had been prepared for cremation and Ramdev, with a heavy heart lit the fire. The crackles of the fire could no longer be heard over the wails and prayers as his last rites were performed. At the end, his ashes would be given to Ramdev and Gayatri. Lakshmi could care less and did not protest the decision.
On the walk back to the house where food and refreshments had been set up for the mourners, Ruby held Lakshmi close and refused to leave her side. Devi had managed to get herself to the front to stand next to Lakshmi on the other side of her and held her tightly. Lakshmi felt relief feeling her mother’s touch. She could never tell Devi of the horrible realities of the life she had led in Kochi or worse, how Vishnu died with a sip of her special cardamom tea that he had once praised to be better than even his own mother’s. That would be too much for her.
Lakshmi also felt Rani’s absence. Rani had seen trailing behind the procession of mourners and made no attempt to come near her. Rani avoided Lakshmi as if she was some sort of plague and Lakshmi knew why, but still wished she had her best childhood friend by her side. Lakshmi knew she hadn’t been in close contact with her friend after her marriage, trying to hide
the reality of her lifestyle from her. Rani likely viewed that as a snub from Lakshmi, now a wealthy woman in Kochi and having no time for her poor friend. The harsher truth unbeknownst to Lakshmi, however, was that Rani’s relatives had stopped her from seeing Lakshmi and sadly, Rani had succumbed to the superstitions of the villagers who saw it as bad luck to associate with a young widow.
“She is just bad luck. She has destroyed poor Gayatri and her family and not only a year of marriage,” a villager muttered in disbelief.
“Pretty she may be, but they should have gone with a girl from their own caste. She is a devious wolf in sheep’s clothing. And not even pregnant after a whole year with his child. Unlucky like her mother,” another woman chipped in.
“Yes, charming and all but you should never marry from a lower caste. It is bad luck to mix between different castes. That’s why God put them at the bottom as punishment for their past life’s sins,” a woman whose daughter had been rejected as a potential bride for Vishnu proudly uttered, loudly enough for Gayatri to hear as well. She was secretly glad that her daughter hadn’t been in Lakshmi place, otherwise this shame would have been her daughter’s misery to bear.
~
Once everyone had gone, Mr. and Mrs. Iyer, Ruby, Dravid and Devi stayed behind. Gayatri refused to acknowledge Lakshmi’s existence. As far as she was concerned, Lakshmi should have burned at the pyre with her husband. Everyone sat there silently in the living room of the grand house in the small village on the outskirts of Idukki hills.
After some time, Mr. Iyer broke the silence and made a suggestion motivated by self-interest and on his wife’s insistence, “Mrs. Iyer and I were thinking that it would make sense that Lakshmi returns with us to Kochi.” Gayatri whose eyes were swollen and red from crying shot him a squinted look full of rage and he stopped dead in his tracks. Mrs. Iyer, however, the shrewd businesswoman ignored this reaction and went on, “Yes, there is no life for a widow here in this village. And it would be a waste for her to be shut off from the world in an ashram for all her life and at such a young age. We take full responsibility for her.”
All Gayatri could think was how the little snake had managed to convince the respectable Iyers into taking her back with them. “No,” Gayatri bellowed, “The little witch will stay at the ashram where she belongs. That is the punishment of a young widow. She killed my son. She killed him that devious bitch. That she-devil.” Lakshmi had anticipated to some extent the insults that she would have to endure and did not react as she sat there quietly absorbing her Maa ji’s abuse in. Ramdev ushered his wife to calm down and control herself. Her sister-in-law tried to hug her and quieten her down, but that only further angered Gayatri who shoved her aside.
Devi sat next to her daughter, hoping that the Iyers’ suggestion would hold some weight with Ramdev and perhaps the village elder. The ashram was no place for her young, beautiful girl. Devi and her husband had lived a life of hardship here in the small village and were ready to take whatever insults the villagers were to throw at them. They had done so before, and they could do it again, but they wouldn’t want such pain to be inflicted on their only daughter.
Grandmother was old too and over the years had hardened herself to that life of gossip and lies. In that moment, Devi
prayed to Goddess Lakshmi. “Please help my dear, sweet daughter. Please help her go back to Kochi and live a full life with the Iyers. She is so young, Goddess Lakshmi, to live the rest of her life like a prisoner. I know you will protect her as you have always done. She is our lucky charm.”
Ruby could see the love for Lakshmi that Devi had in her eyes and prayed for the same for her friend who was like the little sister she never had. Ruby had come from a small town in Punjab and coming back here reminded her of the close-minded thinking that she had escaped by moving to Kochi. It would be such a waste for Lakshmi to stay here. If it came to it, she was determined to kidnap Lakshmi from the ashram and take her back to Kochi. She could then help Lakshmi create a new identity and life for herself, much as she had done for herself.
Gayatri’s glare had moved on from Mr. Iyer to Lakshmi, as Gayatri thought, “She couldn’t just live with him. She wanted all his wealth. She’s probably shacking up with some man she found in Kochi. The snake.” Lakshmi could feel the coldness of Gayatri’s stare on her, a woman that had once been so warm and accepting of her despite her upbringing. “Only if his mother could see what a monster her son was,” Lakshmi quietly thought, “But she was blinded by her maternal love for the scum bag.”
The family sat around grieving quietly and the morning turned from the afternoon and afternoon to evening. Sleeping arrangements were prepared for the guests. Lakshmi, Ruby, Mrs. Iyer and Devi were to share a small spare room with two twin beds and the bathroom in the hallway. Dravid and Mr. Iyer were given a larger room with two double beds and an en-suite bathroom. The decision of Lakshmi’s fate was to be decided in the coming days when tensions cooled and the reality of Vishnu’s sudden passing set in.
~
The arrangement was awkward in the women’s room. The other women insisted that Mrs. Iyer sleep on her own bed. Ordinarily, they would have expected that Mr. and Mrs. Iyer would have been given the larger room, but having Dravid there complicated the sleeping arrangements. After some convincing from Devi and Ruby, Mrs. Iyer got herself into bed. The day had been very long and everyone was tired. Lakshmi had curled into a corner of the other bed and almost looked as though she had disappeared in it.
Devi looked at Ruby and thanked her with her palms pressed together in gratitude. “Aunty, no, no, please. She is like my little sister and you like my mother,” Ruby whispered. They hugged each other acknowledging the long road and pain ahead for their beloved Lakshmi.
“Please. We must not allow her to stay here, Ruby. We must get her back to Kochi. There is no life left here for her,” Devi said as she looked into Ruby’s eyes. Ruby nodded assuredly at Devi. She knew the truth about widows, and understood that it was always a worse life for them in small villages like this one. This village on the outskirts of Idukki hills.
The two women quietly whispered as they got to know one another a little bit more. Ruby swore to herself not to let slip on how life in Kochi had not been as glamorous for Lakshmi as Vishnu and his family had let on. “It is best for her to believe that their daughter will return to a life of luxury in Kochi if we are able to convince the family that Lakshmi comes back home to Kochi with Dravid and me,” Ruby thought to herself. Soon after this, both Ruby and Devi turned in and slept deeply after days of exhaustion.
~
It was in the early hours that Devi was up and getting ready. Mrs. Iyer was snoring loudly, while Lakshmi was also awake and sitting at the edge of the bed looking blankly at the wall ahead. She was lifeless. The stinging words of the villagers pierced through her heart, and the once warm and loving Maa ji was now cold and vengeful. “Goddess Lakshmi, help me escape. I cannot stay behind and live my life in that ashram. I have so much talent and am so young. Please grant me a way out of this small village,” Lakshmi prayed silently as she sat there staring at a spot on the wall. It looked as though she had lost further color in her cheeks overnight.
After Devi was done, Ruby quietly went into the bathroom outside their room to get ready and could hear the members of the household awaken. Cook was already in the kitchen whipping up breakfast to be served in the large living room / dining room. Ruby took further note of how Lakshmi’s in-laws were quite well-to-do, a stark contrast of the life Vishnu had been living in Kochi. The bathroom had a western style toilet and was immaculately clean. By the sink, there were the softest towels that Ruby had ever used and the soaps were scented with jasmine oils.
As she exited the bathroom, she admired the courtyard that was beautifully landscaped and had a handcrafted wooden bench and a delicately carved swing chair positioned in front of the Tulsi plant that sat in the center of the courtyard. A jasmine plant creeped up at the corner of the courtyard and was full of freshly blossoming flowers. The strong but not overpowering scent of the little white flowers permeated throughout the space. As she patted her hair on one end of the courtyard with the Egyptian cotton towel and took in the serenity of the space,
the peacefulness was interrupted with a loud argument that had erupted in the living/dining room space.
She froze in her place as she tried to eavesdrop on the conversation but she couldn’t quite make out what was happening. The other women had also heard the commotion and out came Devi, Mrs. Iyer and Lakshmi closely behind them. They looked at each other, fear taking over their faces as they all quickly made their way to the room where the loud voices of Ramdev and Gayatri could now be heard.
As they approached the room, they saw the village elder and Mr. Iyer sitting with a cup of cardamom tea. Mrs. Iyer glared at him hoping that by doing so she would be able to read his mind. He gulped and looked away from her gaze. Shiva and his wife had also appeared, wondering what all the noise was about.
“Shut up. Shut up,” Gayatri shouted to her husband. “I don’t want to hear another word about my son.”
“But Gayatri. He was working as a driver there. I didn’t tell you because I didn’t want you to be disappointed,” Ramdev tried to tell her in a more reasoned tone, conscious that their argument had brought the whole household into the room. “Mr. Iyer and Mrs. Iyer had helped…” Gayatri’s cold stare put an end to Ramdev’s explanation. Defeated, he sat down.
“You mean, you mean to say,” Gayatri stuttered, “My son was a driver for a sleazy hotel in Kochi. He was a driver like our servant, Vinod.” Her face was full of disgust and disappointment. “You mean there is no luxury apartment in Kochi on Marine Drive? You mean my son was lying to me? You were lying to me?” Seeing Lakshmi come into the room, her lips contorted in contempt as she saw her late son’s wife come in. She stormed out angrily and was unable to face Lakshmi who she had blamed for being a gold digger.
“I should have never interfered,” Mr. Iyer said. “I just thought it was time Gayatri knew the truth and that we could protect her from the disappointment if ever anyone found out the truth.” Mr. Iyer acted innocently. In reality, he and Mrs. Iyer had hatched a plan to make sure they could take their Kochi Queen back to the city and had to act quickly while everyone’s emotions were high. Mrs. Iyer had a knack for strategy and knew this would be the way.
Lakhsmi was too important for the success of the hotel for them to lose her to a stupid village ashram with outdated practices. Mrs. Iyer feigning innocence interrupted and began scolding Mr. Iyer, “How could you have done that? It wasn’t your place to say anything. I’m so sorry, Ramdev ji. We are sorry to both of you. To all of you.” Mrs. Iyer said as she begged forgiveness from Ramdev and then turned to everyone else in the room, bowing her head down, pretending she felt grave guilty.
“No, Mr. and Mrs. Iyer. Please. We are forever indebted to you both for protecting our reputation here in the village. Our son has passed, but he would have been a disgrace if he stayed here with his unruly ways. You saved us from shame when no one else could help us,” Ramdev said.
The village elder had been quiet all this time, observing the situation, but was not surprised at all by this truth. “I always knew that boy was no good. Flashing that apartment and the Hero Honda car,” he started off. Taking charged, he angrily instructed, “But this here needs to be contained in this house and these rooms. We cannot have any of this gossip leak out into the village. Do you know what it would do to my reputation?” The village elder’s angry voice echoed as he pounded the table with his palm. “An absolute disgrace. Good he’s dead and hasn’t brought any more shame to the family.” Everyone was shocked by the village elder’s harsh comment. He never did approve
of Vishnu, but in these circumstances, he had expressed more concern for his position as the village elder than he had for his own son’s feelings.
The village elder then looked squarely at Devi and asked her, “What do you and your family want to be quiet about all of this?” Devi was speechless as this was the first time she had heard a wink of the hardships that her daughter had suffered over the past year. She hadn’t prepared for this moment at all. She couldn’t have. But all of a sudden, she found the words flowing out of her mouth, “My husband and family doesn’t know about this and it’s best to leave it that way, but my daughter will not be confined to an ashram. She is much too young for a life of solitude. Let her return to Kochi with the Iyers and her friend, Ruby, and we will never speak of this again.” Devi was so proud of the voice that came out of her. So confident and decisive.
“Agreed. You are a very sensible woman, Devi. I can’t say the same for my own son and daughter-in-law. The pain that boy gave to our families. He was no good the day he was born. A devil born in our house. A devil. It’s very much a good thing that your daughter and him didn’t spawn any offspring,” the village elder stammered as he got up and left the room. After a few minutes, Ramdev left the room, and so did Shiva and his family.
The Iyers, Ruby, Lakshmi and Devi were left in the room. The Iyers couldn’t hold back and blurted, “We should make plans to go by tonight or tomorrow morning before anyone changes their minds or the village council convinces them to do so otherwise.”
“But what will everyone say. That they only came one day and they left the next?” Devi queried.
Ruby agreed with the Iyers and she didn’t much care for what the small-minded villagers would think. She had promised her friend a way out. “We should leave tonight. Say that we want
to spread his ashes in the Arabian Sea which his apartment overlooks,” Ruby suggested.
Dravid had been missing from the conversation, but through the window in the courtyard, Ruby glanced out in that very moment and she saw a drunk Dravid stumbling in while on the shoulder of Vinod, the driver. Ruby felt angry in that moment. She knew that when he left Kochi he always engaged in sordid activities, but now, while his best friend had just died and she was there with him as well. “Perhaps it was a tribute to that dog, Vishnu,” she thought to herself. “When I get back to Kochi, I’m leaving him once and for all. I deserve better.”
Lakshmi had been silent all this time. Pale from the stress of her impending imprisonment at the ashram, but color started slowly seeping back into her cheeks as the truth had finally come out. Her mother was in complete support of her as well. She also understood what this meant. She could never return to this small village on the outskirts of Idukki hills, but was glad to leave it behind forever. She was deeply saddened by the thought of the shame that her family would have to endure, but she also knew that some new gossip or fancy would come by soon enough and the chatter around their suffering would be replaced by another’s.
Lakshmi looked up in the sky and prayed to Goddess Lakshmi, “Guide me and give me strength to endure the coming days. You have provided an outlet. Please ensure that I will escape.” Tears rolled down her cheeks as she felt a small sense of relief.
As they dispersed from the room, Lakshmi tugged at Ruby and whispered in her ear. “Before we leave, come with me so that I can show you the Gangalee fields that I would lay down in and draw as a child.” Ruby looked down at her dear sweet sister, smiled and nodded. Ruby thought, “How bitter sweet.”
~
That afternoon, Lakshmi and Ruby slipped away from the grand house to the Gangalee fields. It was a bit more of a walk from this house than Lakshmi’s ancestral home, but the pilgrimage was worth it as it would be the last time Lakshmi would set foot here. The girls flopped down in a small clearing, surrounded by the vibrant colors of the Gangalee blossoms. Lakshmi closed her eyes as she absorbed the serenity of the fields.
“I want to capture this moment in my heart forever. I’ll keep this field of beautiful flowers in my mind when I miss my home and my family,” Lakshmi thought to herself as she breathed in the sweet musk that the flowers released in the humid air.
Ruby was equally amazed by the beauty of the flowers that were now in full bloom. These flowers had given Lakshmi her freedom. In looking at her little sister lying there, Ruby thought about the blessings she had received from their friendship. For the first time in her life, she could see that she deserved more than the life she had been living with Dravid, Vishnu and the Kochi Queen. She was ready to take a bold step that she hadn’t thought she deserved. When they got back to Kochi, she would leave Dravid and would live with Lakshmi. After that, she was going to apply to become an air hostess for that airline in Doha that had been advertising. She always had a yearning to see the world and she was no longer going to hold back on that dream.
After a good twenty minutes soaking up the beautiful fields, Ruby tapped on Lakshmi’s shoulder and told her they should head back. Begrudgingly, Lakshmi got up and scanned her surroundings one last time, capturing it in her memory. She bid farewell to the Gangalee blossoms. Lakshmi couldn’t wait to leave that evening. The Iyers had told the family that they would head out then and everything was okayed by the village elder.
“Good riddance” is what the village elder had said with a snarl on his face, remarking again on his grandson. Ramdev didn’t even attempt to interfere with these plans, knowing that their family’s reputation was at stake. Gayatri had been shut up in her room and refused to speak to anyone. Cook had made many attempts to bring her food or cardamom tea, but she refused and yelled at anyone who tried to come into her room.
The hours ticked on. Lakshmi was so nervous as she feared that her plans would be abruptly interrupted. “Please, please, Goddess Lakshmi, get me out of here before it’s too late,” Lakshmi prayed. The women all sat uncomfortably in the room designated for them as they heard the seconds hands on the wall clock tick by.
“Why are we waiting till the evening?” Dravid questioned, eager to leave. He hadn’t been able to face Ruby all day as he could feel the cold, icy energy from her towards him. He had had a moment where he felt he could do the honorable thing and make Ruby his life partner, but coming back to the village reminded him how he was not built to be one of those guys that “settled down”. Like his late friend, Vishnu, he was too immature for a long-term commitment with anyone at the moment. Vishnu had probably been the longest and most meaningful relationship he had ever had. That realization said enough about the type of person he was and he knew it would be a disservice to Ruby to promise anything more than what he already gave her.
Mrs. Iyer responded, “They want us to leave when there aren’t so many villagers out and about.” Mrs. Iyer was realizing the lack of logic in that statement. “But it wouldn’t make any difference. We should go, no?
Devi agreed and in a hushed tone replied, “I think you should leave sooner than later, in case anyone changes their minds.” She looked longingly at her daughter and closed her eyes,
cementing her sweet face in her memory. It was so difficult for her to not know if she’d ever or when she’d see her daughter again, but knew that she had to let her go so that Lakshmi could live a fuller and better life in Kochi than she would have ever had here. Lakshmi looked up at her mother and she ran to her, hugging her tightly, knowing the same. She promised herself that one day when things settled and her brother and her cousin-brother were old enough to live their lives independently, that she’d reunite with her mother and bring her to live in Kochi with her. Lakshmi hadn’t forgotten her dream of one day having that luxury apartment on Marine Drive and was intent on pursuing it at all odds.
Mr. Iyer looked at Mrs. Iyer and then at the group. They fumbled around their luggage, throwing in the last remaining items in, and gathered the suitcases and bags at the entrance of the room. “What about his ashes?” Ruby asked. Mr. Iyer signaled that he would go check with the village elder and Ramdev as the rest of them finished the packing.
About a half hour or so, Mr. Iyer came back and let the group know that they weren’t getting the ashes. “Gayatri ji has locked herself in the room with them. It’s your call, Lakshmi if you would like to stay for the ashes or not.” Lakshmi looked around and then at her mother, who came to her and gave her a long hug good-bye. “It’s your time to live, Lakshmi. This is the hardest thing for me to do in this life, but I cannot let you hold back. You must go now. Forget about his ashes. Why would you want that scumbag’s ashes strewn across the Arabian sea anyway? And don’t worry. The villagers will never stop talking. It is nothing new. Your mother is strong and we will get through this too.”
Lakshmi had received her mother’s blessings to do what she had already willed to do. She could no longer stay here. She felt suffocated in this grand house as it was and she felt so
much happier knowing that Vishnu’s ashes would stay behind, the expanse that had allowed her to breathe freedom and hope. She held her mother with such a tight embrace, once last time, at least for now. She begged her to seek forgiveness from her father, grandmother and siblings for not saying goodbye to them and for laying such a heavy burden at their feet. Her mother stepped back to free Lakshmi from their embrace and patted her back urging her to go now. And with that, Lakshmi, Ruby, Dravid and the Iyers quietly left the house, jumped into the car and left the small village on the outskirts of Idukki hills, back to the life in Kochi.
~
Lakshmi could not wait till to be out of the village limits and as the car inched forward, she slowly felt relief and energy return. She left her mother with the excruciating pain of longing for her, knowing that it would be many years before they would, if ever, be together again. Her mother would have the burden of knowing the lies that her daughter had to endure over the past year and no one would ever know the truth. The Iyers had chosen not to tell her mother that she was the revered Kochi Queen and she was glad for that because her mother would not have let her go back to Kochi in that case.
She was grateful for this opportunity and how things were working out. As she sat there in the car, she thanked Goddess Lakshmi for being so kind and giving her mother the understanding that most in the village would not have had. She was glad her father hadn’t been there as he would have made the situation very difficult for her.
As she sat at the back of the car and thought about being back in her village she had called home for years before, Lakshmi was deeply hurt that Rani hadn’t sought her out or consoled her.
Their friendship had come to an abrupt end as a result of Vishnu and his subsequent death. Lakshmi knew that she was partially responsible for their friendship’s end because she had kept her distance from Rani all these months. Rani knew her too well and so Lakshmi avoided Rani’s calls and slowly they went from calls once every two weeks, to once every three, then every month, to less than that to almost never. Rani knew Lakshmi too well and could always catch her out in a lie, and Lakshmi didn’t want the truth about her life in Kochi exposed to the villagers, especially to her family. It was a sacrifice she was glad she had made now. It would have otherwise spread like wild fire and her family and her would have been disgraced. She was unsure of what truths Maa ji and Papa ji would spread, but either way her reputation would have suffered.
There is no place for a widow in a small village in the Idukki Hills, but only the life of a pariah. She had been branded unlucky, even though it was no fault of her own.  Yes, she had poisoned that oaf, but no one but Ruby knew that, and yet they vilified her for his untimely death.
Lakshmi could see that they were driving on the regional highway now. She sighed deeply and Ruby turned to her and smiled. Ruby was so happy to be leaving that backward village and excited for the life ahead with her dear friend. Once they got back to Kochi, Ruby had vowed to herself that she would break it off with Dravid and start following her dreams, despite the barriers she knew she would face. If she couldn’t make it in Tollywood, she would at least find a way to venture out into the world. That Mideast airline had interviews coming up in the next couple of weeks and she was going to go for it. They were desperate for staff given the shortages, lower pay, and the demands of the work. She had a chance now and although the beauty requirements were quite strict, she had seen all sorts
of women make it through lately. She knew she had a fighting chance if she just tried for it.
~
In memory of Vishnu, Dravid sentimentally stopped at the open-air restaurant that they had taken Lakshmi that first time for biryani. As food was being ordered, Lakshmi looked around thinking, “This once was the mark of wealth for the young, naïve village girl I was.” It was not at all a luxurious eatery as Vishnu had tooted and how innocently she fell into his magic and deception. He had unashamedly fooled her, her family, his own family (except for his proud father) and the entire village, coming there in Mr. Iyer’s car pretending to be some big shot. He was shameless and deserved to die. It made sense why he did not protest his mother’s choice, a lowly village girl. Lakshmi didn’t mind eating at this spot, finally feeling her appetite return. She dug into the biryani and remembered how delicious it had tasted the first time she had it. It tasted so much better and deeply flavorful today. As she sat there, slowly eating the biryani, she looked around the people at her table.
Although she knew the Iyers were motivated by their business interests that had been enhanced by her performances as “Gangalee”, she was grateful that they were in her life, giving her that opportunity. She looked at Dravid. He was scum, but harmless in comparison to Vishnu. He had his vices but had never raised his voice or laid a hand at Ruby or her. She never could figure out his dedication to Vishnu, who was a horrible friend, drunk and abusive most all the time.
Above all, there was Ruby. She felt Ruby was Goddess Lakshmi disguised as her big sister on earth. Ruby had been her savior and had always protected her, even against that awful monster. Despite the circumstances and her becoming a widow, Ruby still
chose to stand by her side and gave her the support she needed to get through this difficult time. Ruby had only known Lakshmi a year, unlike Rani who had known her for a lifetime, but yet, it was Ruby who was by her side now.
And soon after some chit chat and their bellies full, the crew headed back on the road towards Kochi, far away from that village on the outskirts of the Idukki hills. It was dark now, but Lakshmi could see the city lights twinkle as they approached its limits and pure excitement returned.
~
Back in her own apartment, Lakshmi plopped down on that sofa thinking back to the first time she came to Kochi with Vishnu, Dravid and then meeting Ruby. Now, the apartment was sparkling clean, a big contrast to when she had initially set foot in it. It was meant to be a lavish, welcoming marital home overlooking the Arabian Sea, but instead, was a dump nestled in the poor part of the city.
Ruby sat down beside her on the sofa. They were both exhausted, but as they sat there, they both felt energized at the thought of the freedom Vishnu’s death had afforded them. Ruby decided that she had to kick out Dravid and that she would no longer prolong submitting her application to the airline for fear of rejection.
Lakshmi smiled victoriously at her friend. She had broken the cycle that had cursed generations of women before her. She had nothing to lose now and could reinvent herself however she wanted to, now she was in the big city. First Kochi Queen, then Queen of Marine Drive. She had set her sights high and vowed to continue pursuing her dream of owning a 3BKH on Marine Drive, just had she had been promised as that young, innocent
girl with no high school education. Every morning, she would look out onto the Arabian Sea and on her balcony breathe in the calming breeze from the capricious waters.
After some time of sitting in silence, Lakshmi got up to make her special cardamom tea. She looked over at the windowsill in the kitchen and saw that the Gangalee seeds she had planted had started to sprout, which brought her immense joy. “New beginnings, Goddess Lakshmi,” she thought to herself and smiled. She caught Ruby’s worried face and shook her head, then smiled reassuringly. The two women let out a laugh as they basked in their new freedom.
~
Epilogue
"A woman is like a tea bag—you never know how strong she is until she gets in hot water."
~ Eleanor Roosevelt
Lakshmi and Ruby’s friendship blossomed and the two young women each pursued and achieved their dreams. Lakshmi was forever cagey about trusting others outside of Ruby after how Vishnu had treated her, but also how Kalki and Sita acted in their own selfish endeavors. Although Kalki and Sita were motivated by circumstances, they lost out in the end and were kicked out from the Kochi Queen Hotel and blacklisted by the Iyers. Last anyone heard, they ended up as hookers, earning just enough to scrape by on the streets.
Lakshmi did achieve her dream of owning a 3BKH overlooking the Arabian Sea. She had been discovered by a film director who happened by the Kochi Queen Hotel one evening, and the Iyers encouraged her to go ahead to pursue her career in Tollywood. Lakshmi was always grateful for the Iyers’ kindness, even
though they too had been motivated by their own self-interests. She became a famous Tollywood actress, but always returned to her new roots in Kochi. As her star rose, many of her movies were filmed there to accommodate the acclaimed actress.
Sadly, Lakshmi’s mother passed a year after Lakshmi left the small village on the outskirts of Idukki hills, which devastated Lakshmi. Unbeknownst to anyone, she had ovarian cancer that hadn’t been caught in time. Ultimately, it was her mother who had been the catalyst of Lakshmi’s success, encouraging her daughter to leave behind the mundane life of their small village where generations of women like Devi had worked at those cardamom estates. “Goddess Lakshmi, please keep my mother safe and near you always,” Lakshmi prayed every day as she watered her Gangalee blossoms, now planted across her wrap around balcony of her luxury penthouse. A year after her mother passed, her grandmother did too from old age and melancholy having lost her daughter-in-law and knowing the hardships her granddaughter had quietly endured.
Ruby was equally content. She found the courage to leave Dravid, who opted for a life as a non-committal, perpetual drunk. He drank away the grief of losing his best friend and then being rejected by Ruby. His lifestyle landed him on the streets. The Iyers had tried to help him, but after some time, but their ties with him when he no longer seemed to want to sober up and change the old ways he had shared with his old friend, Vishnu.
Although Ruby felt badly, she had to move on for herself. Shortly after they returned, Ruby kicked him out of the apartment and Lakshmi came to live with her. Ruby applied to the airline and after interviewing, she landed a position as ground staff. She slowly worked her way up into cabin crew through her persistence and good nature, which opened up opportunities for
her to fly to new destinations she had never heard of and would have never seen if she hadn’t taken a chance on herself.
When Ruby would return to her homebase in Kochi, she always brought gifts for Lakshmi. Gifts from Doha, Lisbon, Paris, Seoul, Cape Town and more. Having been Lakshmi’s confidant and close friend all these years, Ruby always had a room at that 3BKH of Lakshmi’s ready for her during her layovers. She never had to stay at one of those dingy room shares provided to airline staff.
One evening, she told Lakshmi how she had bumped into Chef Vijaya in Doha. He had gotten a position as a sous chef at one of the top Indian restaurants at a prestigious hotel in Doha and couldn’t have been happier even though his life after the incident had been difficult at first. He was shocked at the callousness of the Iyers, who had been so quick to kick him out without hearing his side of the story and taking Vishnu’s story at face value. Ultimately, he was glad that he had gotten that necessary push, or really the punch to move on. That punch helped him realize his dream of living abroad and he had now settled down with a lovely Tamil lady he had met there and the two had a young son together. He did wonder how Vishnu had passed so suddenly the day after, but Ruby didn’t encourage much discussion around that incident. Regardless, Chef Vijaya, like Lakshmi and Ruby, was glad he was dead and that both ladies had found success in their lives. In fact, their success was all thanks to Vishnu.
Lakshmi never did marry again or have any children of her own. With the passing of her mother and grandmother, Lakshmi permanently shut that chapter of her life of being that girl from that small village on the outskirts of the Idukki hills. Lakshmi had died there and the Gangalee girl had been reborn in Kochi.
